charges by Humana-Sunrise were incomplete as be In 
addition to the $16,437.66 bill that Mr. Egermayer reported, 
Mr. Cade incurred another bill from Humana-Sunrise in 
1987 in the amount of $1,182.60 for outpatient services. 
That was the charge for which Humana-Sunrise billed the 
Cades $146.71, an amount that Mrs. Cade remitted. 


2. Defendants’ estimates regarding the actual amounts of 
co-payments by class members include improper overpayments. 


Humana-Sunrise’s $146.71 charge to the Cades was im- 
proper. According to the terms of the Humana Insurance 
group health policy that covered the Cades, the most they 
were required to pay for covered medical services in 1987 


3. Defendants’ alternative approach to calculating dam- 
ages in terms of the amounts that co-payors actually paid to 
Humana-Sunrise rests on estimates by Defendants that are 
not reliable. In addition, proper calculation of those damages 
requires specific information for each co-payor that Defend- 
ants have never produced during the course of this litigation. 


a. Defendants’ calculations regarding the actual amounts 
of co-payments by class members are not reliable. 


At one time in this litigation Defendants asserted in a 
sworn affidavit that plaintiff Marrietta Cade had paid only 
$87.72 of hospitals bills totaling $16,437.66: 


The records of Humana Health Insurance confirm that 
Robert Cade was hospitalized in October, 1987, at 
Sunrise and that he incurred a hospital bill in the 
amount of $16,437.66. All but $87.72 of that hospital 
bill was covered by the benefits of Group Policy 79201. 


Affidavit of John R. Egermayer, dated May 30, 1989, in 
Defendants’ Supplemental Brief in Opposition to Plaintiffs’ 
Motion for Class Certification. During a deposition of Mrs. 
Cade, Defendants’ counsel Paul R. Hejmanowski represent- 
ed to her as follows: 


Q There’s an affidavit, a written document under oath by 
a representative of one of the defendants who stated, in 
effect, that with respect to Mr. Cade’s October 1987 
hospitalization, that your net payment towards the 
hospital bill was approximately $87.00. 


Cade deposition at 13 (lines 18-23). Mrs. Cade thereafter 
produced copies of her personal checks establishing that she 
had in fact paid $1,146.71 to Humana-Sunrise in 1987 rather 
than the $87.72 that Defendants alleged. The most striking 
aspect of this 1,307% error by Defendants is that if Mrs. 
Cade had not prudently retained her cancelled checks from 


copayments by $146.71. 


This situation may be considerably worse with respect to 
co-payors other than Mrs. Cade. She wisely disregarded 
additional attempts by Humana-Sunrise to collect amounts 
greater than her coinsurance limit. Statements from Huma- 
na-Sunrise demonstrate that on one occasion the hospital 
demanded payment of $353.00 on Mr. Cade’s inpatient 
charges, even though the statement itself indicated the Cades 
had already paid $1,000.00 toward those charges. On anoth- 
er occasion Humana-Sunrise demanded payment of $236.52 
for Mr. Cade’s outpatient charges. As noted supra, any 
charge from Humana-Sunrise for those outpatient services 


JA-164 


was improper, given that the Cades had already reached 
their annual payment limit of $1,000.00 


Other co-payors might have paid those bills, especially 
co-payors recovering from debilitating afflictions, or those 
preoccupied with the task of caring for relatives who were 
recuperating at home, or those in mourning for recently 
deceased relatives. If Mrs. Cade had paid those bills for her 
husband, her overpayment would have totaled $589.52, or 
58.95%. In actual fact, Mrs. Cade paid both the $353.00 
and $100.00 of the $236.52, then demanded refunds. 


If Defendants assert that those overpayments were for 
non-covered expenses, then Defendants are required to 
demonstrate, with respect to each patient, specifically what 
the non-covered expenses were for. Defendants cannot 
merely rely on the sort of entry that appears on Humana 
Insurance’s Explanations of Benefits (EOBs), stating that a 
given charge is for all non-covered items, without identify- 
ing those items and specifying the amount charged for each 
one. As an example, Humana Insurance sent the Cades an 
EOB dated May 5, 1986 that listed $6.05 in non-covered 
charges for ‘‘Hosp Misc’’ without offering any additional 
information. Given Humana Insurance's fiduciary obliga- 
tions to the Cades, a conclusory entry of that sort was 
wholly inadequate, and allowing Humana Insurance to rely 
now on tens of thousands of similar entries would severely 
compound the error. 

Similar examples arose in statements of accounts issued to 
the Cades by Humana-Sunrise. In one statement Humana- 
Sunrise listed a ‘‘total due’’ of $15,437.66, ‘‘estimated 
insurance’ of $15,084.06, and a ‘‘patient amount’’ of 
$353.00. Another statement listed a ‘‘total due’’ of 
$1,182.60, ‘‘estimated insurance’ of $946.08, and a *‘pa- 
tient amount’’ of $236.52. The statements offered no addi- 
tional information concerning the amounts not covered by 
insurance.

1987: $29,065,217

totaled $112,754,512. 

Assuming that the actuarial formula did not include an 
adjustment for any Sunrise Hospital discount, under the 
actuarial formula adopted at the hearing before the Insurance 
Department in 1984, premiums would have to decrease by 
48% if the 89% discount had been constant during this time 
period. Stated another way, since the difference between no

discount and the actual discount given is 89%, and given 
68% of the expenses were allocated to the hospital in the 
actuarial formula (reduced by the assumed 20% non- 
Humana hospital utilization in the actuarial formula because 


If a 48% reduction is applied to these premium charges, 
the total reduction is $54,122,165. It should be noted that 
this figure approximates the total reduction that should be 


| 


the 1984 hearing, premiums would have to decrease by 
32%, if the 89% discount had been constant during this time 
period. More fully, since the difference between a 30% 
discount and a 89% discount is 59%, and 68% of the ex- 
penses were allocated to Humana Hospital in the actuarial 
formula (reduced by the assumed 20% non-Humana hospital 
utilization in the actuarial formula because these projected 
non-Humana hospital billings would not be impacted by the 


JA-168 


‘*secret’’ discount), an additional 59% discount would 
produce roughly a 32% adjustment (68% hospital adjusted 
by 20% non-Humana times 59% discount). 


If a 32% reduction is applied to these premium charges, 
the total reduction is $36,081,443. It should be noted that 
this figure approximates the total reduction that should be 
applied because the evidence suggests that hospital discounts 
were under 89% for a portion of the pertinent time period 
and exceeded 89% during certain pertinent time periods. It 
should further be noted that if the per diem charges for the 
GP-1000 series were $600 per diem as opposed to $125 per 
diem for the HCP-NV-84-1 (which some evidence suggests), 
this damages figure is slightly overstated depending on the 
amount of 1988 premiums allocated to the GP-1000 series 
and the differential between a $125 per diem and a $600 per 
diem. 


In summary, the premium payor damages, without inter- 
est, are $54,122,165.00 if a 30% discount was not passed 
on, and $36,081 ,443.00, without interest, if a 30% discount 
was passed on.

4. Sunrise Is the Most Profitable Hospital on a 
Per Inpatier*t ™ay Basis. 


I have been iafurmed that some representatives from 
Humana claim that Sunrise Hospital is not the most profita- 
ble hospital for the parent company on a per inpatient day 
basis. In other words, I have been informed that some 
Humana representatives have stated because Sunrise has far 
more beds than any other Humana hospital it naturally 
generates more revenue, but when you divide the profit at 
Sunrise by the number of annual inpatient days, Sunrise’s 
profits are much lower than the profits compared to some 
smaller Humana hospitals. 


I have reviewed the Nevada Department of Humana 
Resource’s survey of Humana Hospital chain’s most profita- 
ble hospitals in 1985. Based on my review of the above, and 
the occupancy levels at the listed hospitals in 1985, Sunrise 
Hospital was the second most profitable hospital of all 
Humana hospitals on a per impatient day basis, with the 
exception of Humana’s Aurora, Colorado hospital. 


My calculations indicate that Sunrise generated a profit of 
$238.00 per inpatient day, while Aurora generated a profit 
of $256.00 per inpatient day. The next closest hospital was 
in Lucerne, Florida, generating a profit of only $200.00 per 
inpatient day. However, on review of other Humana internal 
memos provided to the State of Nevada, it was determined 
that Humana’s Aurora, Colorado hospital did not have a 
symbiotic relationship with a Humana Insurance Company in 
1985, and consequently no discounts were provided to the 
Aurora, Colorado hospital. If Sunrise had not given approx- 
imately $5,000,000 in discounts to Humana Insurance in 


JA-181 


1985, then Sunrise would have far outstripped Aurora, 


Colorado as the most profitable hospital per inpatient day in 
Humana’s chain in 1985. 


D. Sunrise Claimed Incremental Costs of $125.00 Per Diem. 


During the deposition of Gary Metcalf, he claimed that 
the $125.00 per diem Sunrise Hospital agreed to accept from 
Humana Insurance, starting in 1986, was sufficient to cover 
the hospital's incremental costs incurred per Humana insured 
inpatient. Other Humana representatives appear to have 
verified this fact, including the Humana Group Health Insur- 
ance Vice President of Finance, Larry Cash. 
I recognize $125 is considered an incremental 
cost. . . .’"; $125 was a rate we adopted to try to make 
sure there would be no profit left in the payment rate 
to the hospitals. 

Cash Deposition at 227. Mr. Cash also testified: 


Q. I guess where this all stemmed from is, you said you 
had an unusual discount arrangement in 1986 because 
you recognized a huge loss in the group health divi- 
sion, correct? 

A. No. What I said was in 1986 we increased the discount 
because we set up a large reserve for losses in the 
future. We increased the discount which brought the 
reserve down and that became the payment rate from 
that point forward. 

The reason we increased the discount was, in our 
opinion we had to eliminate all the potential profit 
from the consolidated into Humana. And if we would 
have kept the payment rate that was adopted or in 
place prior to setting up the reserve, there would still 
have been profit left on the books at hospital so we had 
to eliminate that transaction so it would not look as if 


JA-182 


from an accounting perspective we set up a reserve 
which called for payment rates from the insurance 
company to the hospital and then the hospital then 
make a profit off of that payment rate. 


Cash deposition at 223-24 (emphasis added). 


If in fact Sunrise covered its incremental costs with the 
$125.00 received from Humana Insurance, then any deduct- 
ible paid by the insured working person to Sunrise, and any 
portion of the 20% coinsurance of the full billed charge that 
was profit to the hospital. 

This arrangement would have the effect of allowing the 
hospital to cover its costs and possibly even make some 
profit, while the insurance entity would have a great compet- 
itive advantage over other insurance companies by paying 
far \ess for its insured’s hospitalization.

For example, in a memo dated February 1, 1985, Humana 
Insurance appears to be very concerned that Humana Sunrise 
financial data (through the three-month period ending 12-31- 
84) reflects a net income of $3,500,000.00 after the losses 
from the physician buildings and an allocation of 48% for 
federal taxes.' Keeping in mind that during the 1985 legisla- 
tive session the first real attempt was made to establish 
hospital cost containment regulations, these profits would 
have been $14,000,000 NET OF BUILDING LOSS AND 
TAX. If we compute profit before the 48% tax it is 
$7,300,000.00 for the three months or $29,200,000.00 on 
an annualized basis. Having been personally involved in the 
legislative committee hearings on cost containment in 1985, 
I know that at that time hospitals in Nevada were concerned 
about appearing to have generated high profits. 


In light of the February 1, 1985 memo regarding Sun- 
rise’s very significant profits and the reference to the politi- 
cal realities at the time, I am of the opinion that Sunrise was 
attempting to distort its true profit picture to the State. This

opinion is borne out in the letter dated February 5, 1985

Additional evidence of Humana’s deceptive accounting

Sunrise that stated its parent company charged the hospital 
$4,241,000 for ‘‘home office’’ costs in 1987 and 
$13,058,000 in 1988. Medicare disallowed a substantial 
portion of the 1986 costs. I believe Medicare has disallowed 
or may disallow a substantial portion of the 1987 costs as 
well. Those grossly inflated ‘‘home office’’ charges allowed 
Humana-Sunrise to under-report its profits during those 
years, and demonstrate both deceptive accounting practices 
and abuse of the Medicare program. Similar adjustments 
may be required in cost containment calculations.

mented to appease those hospital managers. In other words, 
the government regulators were apparently given the 
“discount adjusted’’ figures, but the hospital managers got

I have also reviewed the insurance policy of Humana 
Health Insurance of Nevada, noting that the payments are to 
be based on usual and customary charges and that the insur- 
ance company’s payment responsibility is based on paying 
an amount equal to 80% of these usual and customary 
charges. In accordance with the contract and under generally 
would pay an amount equal to the 80% and reflect a claims 
paid expense equal to that amount. Similarly , Humana 
Sunrise would receive payment equal to the 80% and record 
a reduction of that patient's accounts receivable. In fact, 
however, due to the retroactive discounts in 1986 and 
monthly discounts in subsequent years, the insurance 
company’s internal accounting records, as represented by 
Humana and other officials, reflected a substantial! y re- 
duced claims expense and a substantially larger discount. 
According to Larry Cash, Humana increased its discount to 
89.1% effective September 14, 1987. The Annual Statement 
filed by Humana Insurance for that year, however, did not 
reflect that substantially reduced claims expense and substan- 
resulted in a shifting of profits from Humana Sunrise to 
Humana Insurance. In accordance with the contract in effect 
for these period, the recording of these transactions is not 
contractually correct and, therefore, is not in conformity 
with generally accepted accounting principles on a company-

H. Humana Sunrise Benefited Enormously from the 
Secret Discount. 


I have reviewed documents from the State Department of 
Human Resources relative to its computation of compliance 
with AB 289 for 1988. These computations included an 
adjustment in the base year 1986 for the pathology carve-out 
due to its exclusion from 1988 net revenue. An interoffice 
Humana memorandum from Robert B. Leo, Associate 
Executive Director of Finance, to Don Stewart, Executive 
Director of Humana-Sunrise, dated June 10, 1987 
(#178204), noted that ‘“While compliance with 2 25% gross 
revenue reduction is mandated, several objectives/opportuni- 
ties present themselves.’’ Jd. at 1. Mr. Leo identified one of 
those ‘‘opportunities’’ as follows: ‘‘Pathology - Separate 
billing arrangements are currently being negotiated which 
would reduce hospital expenses and revenues.’’ Jd. 

Humana also included an adjustment to add back the 
Humana Insurance Company discounts. However, the dis- 
count added back assumed the GP 1000 policy was the only 
policy in effect for that year, when in fact the GP 1000 
appears to have been a minimal factor in that year, as is 
reflected within the documents sent to the State on March 
27, 1989 by Mr. Egermayer (#12017127-42). In my opin- 
ion, Humana’s misrepresentation of the applicable discount 
in 1988 was an illegal act designed to circumvent AB 289. If 
the State had properly computed these discounts instead of 
relying on Humana’s misrepresentation, then Humana’s non- 
compliance with AB 289 would have been an excess of net 
inpatient revenue approximating $12,500,000. Pursuant to 
AB 289 § 55, subsection 7, the penalty for excessive inpa- 
tient revenues is twice the amount the hospital exceeds its 
required limit, or in this case, $25,000,000.00. 

However, the State’s method of computing the non- 
compliance was done without regard to the increase in pa-

J. Humana’s Bookkeeping Practices and the Application of the 
Secret Discount Appear to be a Kickback. 


As Judge Pro explained in his July 9, 1991 Order, *“The 
agreement between Humana Insurance and Sunrise Hospital 
on which Plaintiffs rely specifically provided for the dis- 
count rate to be the basis of charges to the insurance compa- 
ny, and the usual and customary rate as a continued basis for 
the Plaintiffs’ copayments.’’ (Order at 13). Judge Pro fur- 
Insurance and Sunrise Hospital meant that, although the 
insurance contracts provided for the insureds to cover 20% 
and Humana Insurance to cover 80% of hospital costs 
beyond the deductible, they were no longer percentages of 
the same figure.’’ (Order at 15). 


The above analysis by Judge Pro is consistent, from an 
accounting standpoint, with my understanding of the manner 
in which the fraudulent scheme worked. I have reviewed the 
HCP-NV-84-1 policy and the Humana discount reports to 
verify that, in fact, the discount was applied to the billed 
charges as Judge Pro indicated. In my opinion, and also 
consistent with Judge Pro’s analysis, Humana Insurance 
should have been paying the co-insurance pursuant to the 
insurance contract which clearly stated to the public the 
percentage to be used when paying hospital claims. In my 
opinion, the secret agreement is not an agreement I could 
rely on as a CPA as it was hidden from the buying public 
and strictly between officers of Humana. Accordingly, with 
these facts, I would probably have to conclude that the 
discount is in fact an illegal kickback. 

Furthermore, as a CPA I may have had some obligation, 
if I were doing an independent audit of Humana, to inform 
the proper Humana officer how the discount was being 
treated on the books, and withdraw from the engagement if 
necessary. Since the discount was noted in the audit report, 


JA-199 


the auditors must have been aware of the kickback possi- 
bility. 


I am further informed that Humana Insurance actually 
paid Sunrise 80% of the covered charges through a checking 
account funded for Mutual Administrators, Humana’s third 
party administrator in Nevada. Thereafter, the insured would 
receive an Explanation of Benefits (‘‘EOB’’) report from 
Mutual Administrators, or Humana Insurance, that con- 
firmed to the insured working person that 80% of the cov- 
ered charges were being paid by Humana Insurance. 


This type of deception is a central characteristic of many 
““kickback’’ schemes in which the perpetrator creates a 
"fictitious" billing that is submitted to the victim of the 


canna ad eilieel enieebihand obiies tty hots 
the victim that a different payment arrangement or 
“‘discount’’ was involved. 


Another indicator that this “‘discount’’ was really a kick- 


JA-200 


A zero balance checking account will in fact reduce some 
of the lost ‘‘float’’ (lost use of the money) Humana Insur- 
ance would incur in actually paying 80% of the covered 
claims. However, Humana Insurance would still lose the use 
of its funds for some period of time while the checks made 
their way through the banking system. 

From an economic standpoint, it makes no sense to pay 
80% of a charge when you are responsible for only 11% of 
that charge. There appears to me no economic rationale for 
Humana’s conduct of paying 80% of the Sunrise bill when it 
really knew it would be held responsible for only 11%, 
unless it was attempting to hide that fact from someone. This 
payment charade is further evidence to me that the “‘dis- 
count’’ was really a secret kickback. 

Certain testimony of Humana employees further confirms 
that the ‘‘discount’’ was not a legitimate arms-length trans- 
action, suggesting further that it was really a kickback that 
could be adjusted to whatever level was necessary for 
Humana’s purposes. 

The following is testimony from Samuel Hazen regarding 
the discounts provided by Humana Hospital to Humana 
Insurance. Mr. Hazen was employed at Humana Sunrise 
from August 1987 through May 1989 (Hazen Deposition at 
11). Mr. Hazen testified as follows: 

Q. All right. Now does this document indicate that in July 

of 1988 the total revenue for Humana Sunrise from 
patients insured by Humana Insurance was $426,140? 


A. Yes. 


Q. And does it indicate that there was a discount of 
$661,031? 


A. Yes, this document indicates that. 
Q. That was in July of 1988; is that correct? 


JA-201 


A. Yes. 


Q. Could you calculate for me what percentage of a dis- 
count that is, sir? 

Q. I'll give you few moments if you wish. Would you 
like a pen? 

A. I don’t need a pen. 


Q. Are you calculating that in your head? 


A. Yes. It’s in excess of 150 percent. I don’t have the 
exact amount. 


Hazen Deposition at 39-40. 


In my experience as an accountant for the hospital indus- 
try in the State of Nevada for 24 years, I have never seen a 
case where a hospital gave an 89%, or greater, discount off 
full billed charges. In 1989 the Nevada Department of 
Human Resources reported that the per diems charged to 
imsurance companies by for-profit hospitals in Las Vegas 
other than Humana-Sunrise ranged from a low of $700 to a 
high of $950, far higher than the $125 per diem that 
Humana-Sunrise’s 89% discount equalled. That large a 
discount would not seem to me, based on my experience in 
the industry, to be the result of an arm’s-length negotiation. 
I would also agree with the testimony of Mr. Robert Lub- 
bers wherein he stated: 


Q. O.K. What about the number between July 1, 1987 
and December 31, 1987, for renewing groups with a 
suggest -- or a stated discount or eighty-five point four 
percent; does that also shock you that it’s that high?

some of the goals.

O.K., in my mind. 


Lubbers Deposition, vol, II, at 247-48. 


The fraudulent scheme perpetuated by Humana and 
Humana Insurance is further demonstrated by the fragrant 
inconsistencies between the amount of the discount that 
Humana Hospital reported to the State of Nevada in its 
quarterly reports and the amount of the discount that 
Humana Hospital iater told the State of Nevada Insurance 
Division it had actually applied, as is set forth in greater 
detail above. 


K. Humana Insurance Was Insolvent Without the Secret Discount. 


I am of the opinion that Humana Insurance would not 
have been able to remain solvent but for the secret discount 
agreement. This is additionally evident from the fact that the 
discount increased dramatically in retroactive fashion in 
fiscal year end 1986. It appears from the financial reports 
that Humana Insurance realized that it was taking huge 
losses in 1986 and adjusted the discount in order to stay 
solvent. Larry Cash confirmed this in his deposition also. 
See § D supra. 


I have reviewed the State of Nevada statutes regarding 
required reserves for insurance companies, and I am of the 


JA-203 


opinion that without the secret discount being retroactively 
applied at 89%, Humana Insurance would have met the 
statutory definition of insolvency. This opinion is further 
supported by the rating of the National Association of Insur- 
ance Commissioners (NAIC) in 1986 of Humana Insurance, 
portions of which read as follows: 


A general overview analysis of ratio results and of the 
current year’s and preceding year’s Annual Statement 
has indicated that the company reported a net loss of 
$—1,969,501 and a decrease in capital and surplus 
from $1,432,039 to $358,697. The company’s net in- 
come for the current year was reported as $— 1,969,501 
and included net investment income of $137,357... . 
In the opinion of the Examiner Team, this company 
Should be accorded immediate regulatory attention 

; or worsening surplus and dependence 
upon surplus contribution. . . . 


In her sworn deposition, Teresa Rankin, present Insurance 
Commissioner of Nevada, has stated: 


Q Mrs. Rankin, was there a concern by the Insurance 
Division that Humana Helath Insurance of Nevada, 
Incorporated was in danger of insolvency? 


A Yes there was. . . . I had met with some of our experts 
in the Division and also reviewed the annual statement 
from the prior year, so it would be year ending 
12/31/85 for the domestic insurer, and according to 
my calculations they were about $8,000 above where 
they needed to be. 


In other words, they were in danger of being statu- 
torily impaired on their capital and surplus. 


JA-204 


In addition, their IRIS, which is the Insurancce 
Regulatory Information Service, that is what IRIS 
stands for, those regulations are produced by the 
National Association of Insurance Commissioners 
based on statutory accounting principles and five of 
their ratios were out of line and were not acceptable. 


Rankin deposition at 24 (17) - 25 (11). The Laventhol & 
Horwath report stated at iv: 


The Insurance Company’s operating losses for the 
period from 1984 through 1986 were $35,008 (1984), 


$1,969,501 (1985) and $2,811,737 (1986). . . . The 
inadequacy of the insurance premiums collected during 
the three year period from July, 1984 through Febru- 
ary, 1987 are reflected in these operating losses. Fur- 
thermore, it appears that the Insurance Company would 
not have been solvent during this period of time except 
for capital contributions made by its affiliated ‘‘parent’’ 
company. 


The testimony of David Gates, the Nevada Insurance 
Commissioner during 1986, also confirms that Humana 
Insurance was being scrutinized because of its financial 
condition. However, I understand that the Department of 
Insurance was not aware of the $15,000,000+ retroactive 
discount in 1986, and therefore Humana would have been 
under-capitalized or had insufficient reserves of an additional 
$15,000,000.00 or more than the reported amount, if the 
true financial picture had been presented to the State. 


In addition to the high discount Humana Insurance re- 
ceived from Sunrise, I have also reviewed corporation 
minutes which indicate that the Group Health Division of 
Humana Inc. received additional capital contributions of a 
staggering $164,500,000.00 from June 1986 to June 1987. 


JA-205 


I am also informed that approximately $11,000,000.00 of 
the contributed capital was provided to Humana Insurance in 
order to keep it statutorily solvent. These two factors -- the 
secret discount and the capital contributions -- in my opinion 
kept Humana Insurance from insolvency, thereby allowing it 
to continue to operate in Nevada. By continuing to operate 
Sunrise, Humana could continue to reap great economic 
benefits by reducing its stated profits in an era of legislative 
cost containment, even though, standing along, Humana 
Insurance appeared to be an economic disaster which would, 
by itself, not seem to justify further loss of investment. 


This opinion is confirmed by testimony of Humana offi- 
cials who state that the discount was an integral part of the 
management plan. 


As the foregoing recitation of testimony demonstrates, the 
fundamental objective of the entire Humana Care Plus 
program was to divert patients to Humana Hospital, with the 
apparent understanding that it would cost the parent com- 
pany substantial sums of money. 


The former President of Humana Insurance Company 
stated that Humana Insurance would operate at a loss during 
its first three to five years (Werronen Deposition at 155-56). 
He further stated that Humana Insurance ‘‘had not anticipat- 
ed operating at a profit at the outset’’ (id. at 163). 

The fraud perpetrated through the ‘‘secret’’ discount has 
been described by Humana employees as being an ‘‘inte- 
gral’’ part of the underlying hospital diversion scheme. The 
President of Humana Insurance in 1984 testified that the 
““secret’’ discount was an ‘‘integral part’’ of this manage- 
ment plan (Werronen Deposition at 78-79): 

Q. At some point later on, somebody developed an ar- 

rangement whereby Humana Hospital gave Humana 
insurance Company of Nevada a discount. Was that an 


JA-206 


integral part of the management plan that you prepared 
for the executive committee -- Management Commit- 
tee? 


A. Yes. 


He further stated that the ‘‘secret’’ discount was a funda- 


mental concept of the Humana management plan (id. at 180, 
lines 1-12). 


The President of Humana Insurance has testified that the 
contract with Humana Hospital Sunrise was not developed 
through normal negotiations because it was ‘‘an internal 
arrangement.’’ (Werronen Deposition at 126, lines 15-21): 


The reason I draw that distinction is that the contract 
between Humana and whatever hospital we might have 
contracted with in Reno was a normal document. It was 
developed through rigorous negotiations and so forth. 
The arrangement with Humana Hospital Sunrise was an 
internal arrangement and wasn’t nearly the same proc- 
ess. It was a different animal. 


L. Humana’s Consolidated Profits in Nevada Increased 
During the Class Period. 


As stated in the preceding paragraph, although Humana 
Insurance may have lost money in Nevada, there appears to 


have been a significant benefit to the parent company ih 
creating the insurance entity in spite of the losses it incurred. 

I reviewed the quarterly financial reports submitted by 
Sunrise to the State of Nevada, along with certain other 
financial documents referred to in Section I. hereinabove, 
and based on that review concluded that Humana, Inc.’s 
consolidated profit derived from Nevada operations in- 
creased dramatically from 1985 to 1989, as set forth below:

to believe.

MOTION FOR LEAVE TO FILE 
THIRD AMENDED COMPLAINT AND JURY DEMAND 


Plaintiffs, by and through their counsel of record, Jones, 
Jones, Close & Brown, Chartered, hereby file this Motion 
for Leave to File Third Amended Complaint and Jury 
Demand. This Motion is based on Fed. R. Civ. P. 15, the 
pleadings and papers on file with the Court, and the points 
and authorities set forth herein. 


POINTS AND AUTHORITIES 
I 


STATEMENT OF THE CASE 


On March 29, 1989, Plaintiffs filed their initial complaint 
in this matter. On April 28, 1989, Defendants filed a Motion 
to Dismiss that Complaint. On September 27, 1989, this 
Court entered an Order effectively denying Defendants’ 
Motion to Dismiss. On October 27, 1989, Plaintiffs filed an 
Amended Complaint in response to the rulings in that Order. 
On January 18, 1990, Defendants filed a Second Motion to 
Dismiss. On July 9, 1991, this Court entered an Order effec- 
tively denying that Second Motion to Dismiss. In that Order 
the Court granted Plaintiffs leave to file a Second Amended 
Complaint, and on August 12, 1991, Plaintiffs filed their 
Second Amended Complaint. On September 4, 1991, De- 
fendants filed their first Answer. 


JA-210 


Il 
STATEMENT OF THE LAW 


Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a) allows a party to apply for leave to 
amend a pleading, and provides that “‘leave shall be freely 
given when justice so requires.’ One authority has observed 
that ‘‘[s}ubdivision > ene See 
bly on requests to amend . This proposition has been 
reinforced by innumerable judicial pronouncements by the 
federal appellate and district courts alike.’” 6 Charles A. 
Wright, Arthur R. Miller, & Mary K. Kane, Federal Prac- 
tice and Procedure § 1483 (1990). Most significantly, the 
United States Supreme Court declared in Foman v. Davis, 
371 U.S. 178 (1962), that 


If the underlying facts or circumstances relied upon by 
a plaintiff may be a proper subject of relief, he ought to 
be afforded an opportunity to test his claim on the 
merits. In the absence of any apparent or declared 
reason . . . the leave sought should, as the rules re- 
quire, be ‘‘freely given.”’ 


Id. , 371 U.S. at 182. See also, e.g., United States v. Webb, 
655 F.2d 977 (9th Cir. 1981); Hurn v. Retirement Fund 
Trust, Etc., 648 F.2d 1252 (9th Cir. 1981). In Howey v. 
United States, 481 F.2d 1187 (9th Cir. 1973), the Ninth 
Circuit reversed a denial of leave to amend, even five years 
after the initial complaint was filed, in the absence of preju- 
dice to the defendant. 

Plaintiffs’ proposed Third Amended Complaint is addi- 
tionally a supplemental pleading pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 
15(d). One of the reasons for the adoption of paragraph (d) 
is that otherwise a plaintiff might be forced into *‘commenc- 
ing a new action even though events occurring after the 
commencement of the original action have made clear the 
right to relief.’’ Fed. R. Civ. P. 15 advisory committee 
notes (1963). That is precisely the situation here. 


JA-211 


1. Allegations regarding Defendants’ RICO conspiracy. 


Plaintiffs file this Third Amended Complaint to allege 
violations of 18 U.S.C.§ 1962(d), i.e., a conspiracy to 
violate 18 U.S.C. §§ 1962(a) and (c) of the federal RICO 
statutes. In its Order of July 9, 1991, this court dismissed a 
portion of Plaintiffs’ RICO claims pursuant to 18 U.S.C. § 
1962(a). The Court adopted the rule of some federal circuits 
that Plaintiffs were required to establish an injury by reason 
of the use or investment of racketeering income. The Court 
concluded that Plaintiffs ‘‘have not alleged injury resulting 
from investment of racketeering income, and could not do so 
even if they had the chance to amend.’’ Order at 38. 


On August 16, 1990, after Plaintiffs had filed both thei: 
Ninth Circuit issued an opinion in Reddy v. Litton Indus., 
Inc., 912 F.2d 291 (9th Cir. 1990), cert. denied, 60 
U.S.L.W. 3057 (October 15, 1991) that addressed the re- 
quirements for establishing a conspiracy to violate 18 
U.S.C. 1962(a), pursuant to the conspiracy portion of the 
RICO statutes, 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d). The court held: ‘‘[A] 
plaintiff must have been injured by overt acts that are them- 
selves statutorily defined acts of racketeering in order to 
have standing under § 1962(d),”’ id. at 295—i.e., by the 
predicate act itself, and not necessarily by the use or invest- 
ment of racketeering income. In other words, the injury 
requirement for alleging a conspiracy to violate 18 U.S.C. 
§ 1962(a), (b), or (c) leapfrogs those intermediate provisions 
and concentrates on the predicate act itself. Accordingly 
Plaintiffs now wish to amend their Complaint to allege that 
> arene apg ga 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a) and 
c). 


JA-212 


2. Allegations regarding Wendell Cherry and William Larry Cash. 

The proposed Third Amended Complaint additionally 
joins two culpable parties to this litigation: the estate of 
Wendell Cherry and its administrator, and William Larry 
Cash. Plaintiffs’ grounds for joining those Defendants are 
set forth at length in the attached Third Amended Complaint 
5-6 and passim. 

3. Absence of prejudice to Defendants. 

Granting Plaintiffs leave to file this Third Amended 
Complaint will not unduly prejudice defendants, for the 
following reasons: 


—Plaintiffs allege no new operative facts. The same 
transactions, occurrences, relationships, and injuries that 
animated Plaintiffs’ earlier complaints remain at the core of 


—The additional co-Defendants were knowing, willful, 
and active participants in the scheme to defraud. Hence they 
were aware of the transactions, occurrences, relationships, 
and injuries alleged in this litigation even long before Plain- 
tiffs filed their initial Complaint. Moreover, those additional 
co-Defendants were aware of this litigation itself from the 
outset thereof and of Plaintiffs’ allegations therein. 


—The parties to this litigation have engaged in relatively 
few essential depositions. By far the larger portion of those 
essential depositions remain to be scheduled. Plaintiffs alone 
presently contemplate scheduling more than twenty-five dep- 
ositions hereafter. 

—More than three months remain in this litigation for 
discovery efforts and motions. That is entirely adequate for 
Defendants to pursue all necessary discovery activities. 


JA-213 


For all of the foregoing reasons, Plaintiffs respectfully 
request leave to file a Third Amended Complaint and Jury 
Demand in conformance with Exhibit 1.

JURISDICTION AND VENUE 


1. This action arises under the laws of the United States 
and includes claims for relief under 29 U.S.C. § 1001, et 
(‘‘ERISA’’); these proceedings are also instituted against the 
above-named Defendants Humana, Inc. and Humana Health 
Insurance, Inc. under Sections 4 and 16 of the Clayton Act 
(15 U.S.C. §§ 15 and 26) to secure damages from and 
injunctive relief against Humana for its violations of Sec- 
tion 2 of the Sherman Act (15 U.S.C. § 2) as hereinafter 
alleged; these proceedings are also instituted under 18 
U.S.C. §§ 1961 - 1968; these proceedings are also instituted 
under the law of the State of Nevada. Subject matter juris- 
diction is conferred on this Court by 15 U.S.C. §§ 15 & 16, 
18 U.S.C. § 1964(c), 28 U.S.C. § 1331, 28 U.S.C. § 1337, 
28 U.S.C. § 1367, and 29 U.S.C. § 1132 

2. Venue of this action is proper in this Court pursuant to 
15 U.S.C. § 15, 18 U.S.C. § 1965, 28 U.S.C. § 1391, and 
29 U.S.C. § 1132.

offices and transact business and may be found within the

13. Defendant Humana Health Insurance of Nevada, Inc.

(‘‘Group Health’’). Humana Insurance markets and sells 
group health insurance through employee benefit plans to 
employers throughout the State of Nevada, but with its 
principal geographic market being the Southern Nevada 
market, an area that includes portions of Southern Califor- 
nia, Arizona, and Utah. 


14. Defendant Humana, Inc. (‘‘Humana’’) is a corpora- 
tion organized and existing under the laws of the State of 
Delaware, with its principal place of business in Louisville, 
Kentucky. Humana is, and has been for many years, one of 
the largest owners and operators of acute-care hospitals in 
the United States. It owned and operated approximately 87 
hospitals and health care facilities in 1986, one of which was 
Humana Hospital-Sunrise (‘‘Humana-Sunrise’’) in Las 
Vegas, Nevada. Although Humana-Sunrise was only one of 
Humana’s 87 hospitals in 1986, according to Humana’s 
1986 report to stockholders, Humana-Sunrise generated 6.6 
percent of Humana’s gross revenue; and according to 
documents filed with the Nevada Department of Humana 
Resources in 1986, Humana-Sunrise generated 38 percent of 
Humana’s pretax profits. 


JA-227 


15. Defendant Wendell Cherry was at all times relevant to 
this litigation a resident of Kentucky. Cherry was one of the 
two co-founders of Humana, Inc. During the years 1984 
through 1989, Cherry was the President and Chief Operating 
Officer of Humana, Inc., a member of the Executive 
Committee of Humana, Inc., a director of both Humana, 
Inc. and Humana Insurance, and a principal stockholder of 
Humana, Inc. Cherry died on or about July 16, 1991. First 
Kentucky Trust Co. has been duly appointed as administra- 
tor of the estate of Wendell Cherry. Defendant William 
Cherry and Defendant First Kentucky Trust Co., in its 
capacity as the administrator of the estate of Wendell 
Cherry, are hereinafter referred to collectively as ‘‘Wendell 
Cherry”’ or ‘*Cherry.”’ 

16. Defendant William Larry Cash is and was at all times 
relevant to this litigation a resident of Kentucky. Cash is and 
has been an employee of Humana, Inc. and/or Humana 
Insurance since at least 1973. During the period from 1983 
through 1989 Cash held the position at first of Vice 
President of Finance and Controller, Group Health Division, 
Humana, Inc., and later of Senior Vice President of 
Finance, Group Health Division, Humana, Inc. From 1984 
through at least 1989 Cash held the position of Vice Presi- 
dent of Humana Insurance. From 1986 through at least 1989 
Cash was a director of Humana insurance.

designated as DOES I through X, inclusive, are not known

by fictitious names. Each of those Defendants is responsible 
in some manner for the events and happenings and

B. Concealment of the Secret Discount

reduction or discount to the charges it billed insureds for 
those services; and that Humana Insurance was not passing 
along to insureds the savings that Humana Insurance ob- 
tained by negotiating a discount on those charges for treat- 
ment of those insureds at Humana-Sunrise.

returning the benefit to the Co-Payor Class. As a result of 
the scheme, Defendant Humana Insurance was unjustly 
enriched, all to the detriment of the Co-Payor Class.

Humana Insurance.

Vv 
SECOND CLAIM FOR RELIEF 
(Violations of Federal Antitrust Laws) 


141. Plaintiffs reassert and reallege each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs 1 through 140 of this 
Third Amended Complaint as though fully set forth at this 
point. 

142. The Antitrust Premium-Payer Class consists of 
individuals or entities paying all or a portion of the Humana 
Insurance premiums during the period from 1985 through 
1988, including without limitation employers who made 
insurance premium payments on behalf of their employees, 


as well as individual employees who either directly paid 
insurance premiums on behalf of their dependents, or who 


did so indirectly by such means as payroll deductions by 
their employers. 


143. Defendant Humana is in the business of owning and 
operating for-profit acute-care health facilities. Humana 
owns and operates a for-profit acute-care health care facility 
named Humana Hospital-Sunrise which provides hospital 
services in Las Vegas, Nevada. 

144. Defendant Humana is also in the business of owning 
and operating group health care insurance companies, and in 
1984 established one such company, Humana Insurance, 
with its principal place of business in Nevada. 

145. The relevant geographic market for antitrust pur- 
poses is Clark County, Nevada or a portion thereof. 


146. The relevant product market affected by Defendants’ 
conduct is the sale of general, non-specialty, for-profit, 
acute-care hospital services or a portion thereof, and the 
group health insurance or health care financing market or a 
portion thereof. 


JA-259 


147. Humana-Sunrise has possessed and exercised market 
power in the relevant geographic market during the class 
period. One indication of Humana-Sunrise’s market power is 
its ability consistently to generate profit levels far above 
competitive levels found in comparable geographic markets, 
or as compared to national averages, or as compared to 
Humana hospitals located in other states. In addition to 
exercising this power within the market for the sale of 
general, non-specialty, for-profit, acute-care hospital serv- 
ices, the market power of Humana-Sunrise is heightened 
within relevant submarkets that may include only privately- 
owned hospitals, or other submarkets that focus on particular 
hospital products or services, especially as those products or 
services are obtained by private insureds. 

148. Since at least 1984, and to and including the date of 
the filing of this Complaint, Humana and Humana Insurance 
have violated Section 2 of the Sherman Act (15 U.S.C. § 2) 
by monopolizing and attempting to monopolize the above- 
described markets and/or submarkets. 


149. In furtherance of the aforesaid monopolization and 
attempt to monopolize, Defendants formed a symbiotic 
relationship between Humana-Sunrise and Humana Insur- 
ance, with Humana Insurance acting as a feeder mechanism 
to funnel Humana Insurance insureds into Humana-Sunrise, 
where those insureds became members of the Antitrust Co- 
Payor Class. Defendants established and maintained that 
relationship with the express purpose and effect of 
maintaining or enhancing the market power of Humana- 
Sunrise. 


150. Humana and Humana Insurance engaged in a scheme 
that consisted of numerous actions, the primary purpose and 
effect of which was to maintain or enhance the market 
power of Humana-Sunrise. 


JA-260 


151. As part of the aforementioned scheme, Humana- 
Sunrise Hospital and Humana Insurance entered into a secret 
discount arrangement. Humana and Humana Insurance did 
not offer that discount arrangement to other insurance 
companies or to other general, non-specialty, for-profit, 
acute-care hospitals in the relevant geographic market, did 
not include Humana Insurance co-payers in that discount 
arrangement, and did not pass along to Humana Insurance 
co-payors the savings that Humana Insurance obtained from 
that discount arrangement. That discount arrangement addi- 
tionally allowed Humana-Sunrise to conceal illegal profits. 


152. As part of the aforementioned scheme, Humana and 
Humana Insurance prepared and disseminated, or caused to 
be prepared and disseminated, advertisements that stated 
Humana Insurance was passing along savings to its insureds, 
yet failed to disclose to those insureds that Humana-Sunrise 
had agreed to accept as payment in full, for treatment at 
Humana-Sunrise of persons insured by Humana Insurance, 
amounts that were far lower than the amounts that Humana 
stated it charged for those services, and that Humana-Sunrise 
was not to apply any reduction or discount to the charges it 
billed insureds for those services; and that Humana Insur- 
ance was not passing along to insureds the savings that 
Humana Insurance obtained by negotiating a discount on 
those charges for treatment of those insureds at Humana- 
Sunrise. Those advertisements conferred an unfair competi- 
tive advantage on Defendants. 

153. As part of the aforementioned scheme, Humana 
Insurance sales personnel did not inform insureds that 
Humana Insurance was not passing along to them the savings 
that Humana Insurance obtained by negotiating a discount on 
the charges for treatment of those Plaintiffs at Humana- 
Sunrise. 


JA-261 


154. As part of the aforementioned scheme, Humana and 
Humana Insurance hospital engaged in additional actions, 
including without limitation the following: 

A. Defendants promoted the use of services offered 
by Humana-Sunrise and discouraged the use of services 
offered by other general, non-specialty, for-profit, 
acute-care hospitals through Humana Insurance poli- 
cies, by requiring extensive penalties for insureds who 
obtained services from general, non-specialty, for- 
profit, acute-care hospitals other than Humana-Sunrise. 
Those penalties included without limitation the fol- 
lowing: 

1. Requiring a different and punitive deductible 
for obtaining services from general, non-specialty, 
for-profit, acute-care hospitals other than Humana- 
Sunrise; 


2. Requiring a different and punitive co-pay- 
ment for obtaining services from general, non- 
specialty, for-profit, acute-care hospitals other than 
Humana-Sunrise; 


3. Applying a different standard of review when 
evaluating and paying claims from general, non- 
specialty, for-profit, acute-care hospitals other than 
Humana-Sunrise. 


B. Humana engaged in a pattern of predatory pricing 
by offering Humana Insurance an unreasonable dis- 
count, while not offering that discount to other group 
health insurers in the relevant geographic market, nor 
to members of the Antitrust Co-Payor Class; 

C. Humana Insurance required premiums for group 
health insurance policies that were anti-competitively 
high in comparison to premiums charged for compara- 


JA-262 


ble insurance by other group health care insurers in 
comparable geographic markets; 

D. Humana Insurance initially engaged in predatory 
pricing within the relevant geographic market by at- 
tempting to undercut the premiums required for com- 
parable insurance by other group health insurers in that 
market. 


E. Humana Insurance required premiums for group 
health insurance policies that were far higher than those 
that normal actuarial practices would have indicated 
were reasonable for the percentages and amounts of co- 
payments of Humana-Sunrise’s actual charges that 
members of the Antitrust Co-Payor Class paid. 


155. The aforementioned scheme created misinformation 


among employers, insureds, and other third-party payers 
potential discounts, thereby blunting competitive forces and 
allowing Humana-Sunrise to charge higher prices and offer 
less discounts than it could have without the scheme. In 
addition, because Humana Insurance misrepresented the 
risk-sharing provisions of its group health insurance plan, 
the premiums that Humana Insurance charged were artificial- 
ly high, notwithstanding that at times those premiums may 
have been priced nominally lower than the premiums of 
comparable policies within the Clark County, Nevada area. 


156. As a direct and proximate result of Defendants’ 
market position and power, of their specific intent to 
maintain or enhance that market power, and of their actions 
to maintain or enhance that market power with a dangerous 
probability of success, the members of both the Antitrust 
Premium-Payor Class and the Antitrust Co-Payor Class 
incurred antitrust injuries and damages. The members of the 
Antitrust Premium-Payor Class incurred antitrust injuries 
and damages in that they were required to pay premiums for 


JA-263 


Humana Insurance policies that were high in comparison to 
what competitive rates would have been in the absence of 
Humana’s market power. The members of the Antitrust Co- 
Payor Class incurred antitrust injuries and damages in that 
they were required to pay co-payments for Humana-Sunrise 
services that were high in comparison to what competitive 
rates would have been in the absence of Humana’s market 
power. 

157. Plaintiffs estimate that the approximate actual 
damages of the Antitrust Premium-Payor Class and the 
Antitrust Co-Payor Class are in excess of $181,034,570.00, 
the principal of which is subject to trebling. 

158. The anticompetitive acts and practices of Humana 
and Humana Insurance as set forth and described herein are 
reasonably likely to continue in the future and to cause 


further injury and damage to Plaintiffs unless restrained by 
order of this Court.

164. As part of a scheme to defraud Plaintiffs and to 
obtain money from Plaintiffs by false pretenses, by charging 
Plaintiffs a different per diem per patient day on their co- 
payments, Humana and Humana Insurance entered into a 
secret agreement (the ‘“‘Agreement’’) whereby Humana- 
Sunrise agreed to accept as payment in full, for treatment at 
Humana-Sunrise of persons insured by Humana Insurance, 
amounts that were far lower than the amounts that Hu- 
mana stated it charged for those services, and whereby 
Humana-Sunrise was not to apply any reduction or discount 
to the charges it billed insureds for those services. Humana 
Insurance did not discount the portion of those charges paid 
by Plaintiffs but based them instead on the usual and cus- 
tomary rate. 


165. Humana Insurance did not reduce premiums in the 
amount discounted. As a part of the scheme to defraud and 
to obtain money by false pretenses, Humana, Humana 
Insurance, Wendell Cherry, and/or William Larry Cash used 
false and fraudulent pretenses, representations, and promises

secret agreement (the ‘‘Agreement’’). Pursuant to the 
Agreement, Humana Insurance agreed to pay Humana- 
Sunrise a discounted amount for that portion of the hospital 
charges for which it was responsible. The portion of the 
charges paid by others, however, was not discounted but 
was still based on the usual and customary rate.

amount discounted. It was part of the scheme to defraud and

Insurance, Wendell Cherry, and/or William Larry Cash used

calculated to deceive persons of ordinary prudence and due

that Humana was billing Humana Insurance at a different per 
diem, all so as to unlawfully, intentionally, and willfully, 
and with the intent to defraud, that is, knowingly and with 
specific intent to deceive in order to cause financial gain to 
themselves, procure secret profits to the use and benefit of 
themselves and others and to the detriment of the insureds 
consisting of the named Plaintiffs, as representatives of a 
class pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 23.

broad objectives of Humana and Humana Insurance were

C. The RICO “Enterprise” 


231. Plaintiffs allege three alternative ‘‘enterprises’’ 
within the meaning of Nev. Rev. Stat. § 207.380, 
§ 207.400(1)(aX(2), and § 207.400(1)(c), and, with respect 
to each of those alternatives, an alternative ‘‘person’’ or 
““persons’’ within the meaning of Nev. Rev. Stat. 
§ 207.400(1), § 205.380, and § 686A.291, as set forth 
below. 

232. Each enterprise was engaged in interstate commerce 
during the relevant times, and the activities of each enter- 
insurance for medical services, to persons who were resi- 
dents of states other than Nevada. In furtherance of their 
chased from vendors located in states other than Nevada 
equipment and supplies that were delivered to Defendants’ 
offices in Nevada from those other states by means of inter- 
State transportation. In furtherance of their business activi- 
ties, Defendants used the United States mails and interstate 
telephone and wire communications. 


1. Humana as RICO “‘Enterprise”’ but not as RICO ‘‘Person’’

Nev. Rev. Stat. § 207.380, § 207.400(1)(a)(2), and 
§ 207.400(1)(c).

207 .400(1)(c).

PRAYER FOR RELIEF 


WHEREFORE, Plaintiffs pray for judgment against 
Defendants, jointly and/or severally, where applicable in 
each claim for relief as follows: 


1. An amount in excess of $84,520,143.00 based on the 
ERISA violations by Humana Insurance, Wendell Cherry, 
and William Larry Cash on the First Claim for Relief. 


2. Imposition of a constructive trust in the amount of all 
monies received as a result of the scheme, with Plaintiffs to 
be named as the beneficiaries of the trust on the First Claim 
for Relief. 


3. Declaratory judgment and permanent injunction estab- 
lishing the rights and duties between the parties and for 
judgment requiring Humana Insurance and William Larry 
Cash to cease and desist from the wrongful conduct de- 
scribed in the First Claim for Relief. 


4. General, special, and compensatory damages in an 
amount in excess of $181,034,570.00 on the Second Claim 
for Relief, which damages are subject to trebling (excluding 
interest) for a total amount in excess of $421,426,569.00. 


JA-304 


5. For declaratory judgment and permanent injunction 
requiring Defendant to cease and desist the wrongful conduct 
described in the Second Claim for Relief. 


6. General, special, and compensating damages in an 
amount in excess of $181,034,570.00 on the Third Claim 
for Relief, which damages are subject to trebling (excluding 
interest) for a total amount in excess of $421,426,569.00. 


7. General, special, and compensating damages in an 
amount in excess of $181,034,570.00 on the Fourth Claim 


for Relief, which damages are subject to trebling (excluding 
interest) for a total amount in excess of $421,426,569.00. 


8. Costs of suit and fees incurred herein. 

9. Any further and additional relief that this Court may 
deem just and equitable.

CERTIFICATION 
I, William E. Lawrence, certify the following: 


1. I am Vice President - Telecommunications for Humana 
Inc. and have been responsible for arranging local and long 
distance telephone services for Humana Inc. and its subsidi- 
aries for eleven (11) years. 


2. I have reviewed the Plaintiff's Fifth Request for Pro- 
duction of Documents in the case styled Mary Forsyth, et al. 


vs. Humana Inc., et al., Case No. CV-S-89-249-PMP _ 


(LRL). 


3. Long distance telephone records of Humana Inc. for 
the period 1984 through 1988, including those records of 
long distance calls made between the corporate office of 
Humana Inc. in Louisville, Kentucky and Humana Hospital- 
Sunrise and Humana Health Insurance of Nevada’s market 
office, both located in Las Vegas, Nevada, are maintained in 
the form of copies of bills or invoices from various long 
distance carriers, and are in storage in Louisville, Kentucky. 

4. In preparation to respond to Plaintiff's Fifth Request 
for Production of Documents, I reviewed several selected 
months’ internal reports summarizing Humana’s long dis- 
tance telephone usage. Based upon my analysis of those 
records, I have estimated that for the period 1984 through 
1988, approximately fourteen million (14,000,000) long 
distance telephone calls were made from Humana’s corpo- 
rate offices in Louisville, Kentucky to locations in Kentucky 
and other states, including calls made to Humana Hospital- 
Sunrise and Humana Health Insurance of Nevada’s market 
office located in Las Vegas, Nevada. Also, among that 
estimated total are long distance telephone calls that origi- 
nated at Humana Hospital-Sunrise and Humana Health 
Insurance of Nevada's market office in Las Vegas, Nevada, 
to other locations in Nevada and to locations in other states, 


JA-311 


including calls to the corporate offices of Humana Inc. in 


I hereby certify that the foregoing is true and accurate to 
the best of my knowledge, information and belief.

Re: Transfer of claims processing and payment from Mutual 
Administrators to Humana Corporate Claims System, 
Louisville, Kentucky. 

Dear Bob, 


The primary purpose for the above transfer is to bet*:-r serve 
the consumer by providing them the claims, utilizator. and 
trend reports on a regular basis. By providing these re “ts, 
it will enable the consumer to better manage health care 
costs and provide a review of when employees and depend- 
ents are using health care services. These kinds of reports 
currently are not able to be generated on a regular basis 
through Mutual Administrators, Inc. Also, this will enable 
Humana Health Insurance of Nevada, Inc. to better comply 
with the recent proposed legislation in reporting data on 
health costs to the Department. The following is an outline 
of notification and implementation: 
1. Notification: 
(a) Effective data of termination of contract is Decem-

RUDOLPH KERRY WHALEN, being first duly sworn,

3. In 1986 my wife was hospitalized at Humana Hospital-

dependent pursuant to a policy of health insurance issued by 
Humana Insurance Co. of Nevada. Consequently I am a 
Plaintiff in this action.

thereby causing Humana Hospital-Sunrise to demand that | 
pay those bills.

and Humana Insurance Co. of Nevada and/or its agent, 


JA-316

ATTORNEYS FOR PLAINTIFFS 


UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
DISTRICT OF NEVADA 


CASE NO. CV-S-89-249-PMP (L.R.L.) 


MARY FORSYTH, et. al. 


Plaintiffs, 
vs. 


HUMANA, INC.., et, al. 
Defendants. 


PLAINTIFFS’ MOTION FOR LEAVE TO FILE 
REVISED THIRD AMENDED COMPLAINT AND JURY DEMAND 


Plaintiffs, by and through their counsel of record, Jones, 
Jones, Close & Brown, Chartered, hereby file this Motion 
for Leave to File Revised Third Amended Complaint and 
Jury Demand. This Motion is based on Fed. R. Civ. P. 15, 
the pleadings and papers on file with the Court, and the 
points and authorities set forth herein. Plaintiffs have 
drastically altered their former proposed Third Amended 
Complaint, deleting all new parties and any new claims 
other than Plaintiffs’ 18 U.S.C. § 1503 allegations. These 
measures should eliminate the concerns expressed by the 
Court in its Order of December 6, 1991. 


JA-322 


POINTS AND AUTHORITIES 
I 
STATEMENT OF THE CASE 


On March 29, 1989, Plaintiffs filed their initial Complaint 
in this matter. On April 28, 1989, Defendants filed a Motion 
to Dismiss that Complaint. On September 27, 1989, this 
Court entered an Order effectively denying Defendants’ 
Motion to Dismiss. On October 27, 1989, Plaintiffs filed an 
Amended Complaint in response to the rulings in that Order. 
On January 18, 1990, Defendants filed a Second Motion to 
Dismiss. On July 9, 1991, this Court entered an Order effec- 
tively denying that Second Motion to Dismiss. In that Order 
the Court granted Plaintiffs leave to file a Second Amended 
Complaint, and on August 12, 1991, Plaintiffs filed their 
Second Amended Complaint. On September 4, 1991, De- 
fendants filed their first Answer. 


On October 31, 1991, Plaintiffs filed their Motion for 
Leave to File a Third Amended Complaint (Exhibit 1). On 
November 15, 1991, Defendants filed their Opposition to 
that Motion (Exhibit 2). On November 25, 1991, Plaintiffs 
filed their Reply to that Opposition (Exhibit 3). On Decem- 
ber 6, 1991, the Court issued an Order denying Plaintiffs’ 
Motion (Exhibit 4). 

ll 


A. Plaintiffs’ Revised Third Amended Complaint Remedies the 
Stated Concerns of This Court. 


In its Order denying Plaintiffs’ first Motion for Leave, the 
Court stated: 


[T]he Court finds that Plaintiffs have failed to demon- 
strate that the proposed additions to the complaint could 
not have been included in earlier amendments. Plain- 


JA-323 


tiffs have had numerous opportunities to apprise De- 
fendants and this Court of all purported grounds for 
relief and any additional parties they to bring before the 


a. Federal RICO violations of 18 U.S.C. 1962(d) con- 
spiracy to violate 18 U.S.C. 1962(a); 


b. Federal RICO violations relating to 18 U.S.C. § 1503. 
c. Violations of the Nevada RICO statutes; 


d. Violations by additional Co-Defendants Wendell 
Cherry and William Larry Cash. 


tiffs’ cis regarding Vans of 18 USC § 1503, on 


JA-324 


judicial economy those claims ought to be joined in the 
present litigation. 

In all other respects the revised Third Amended Com- 
plaint, now less than one-half the length of its predecessor, 
contains only the technical modifications and clarifications 
that appeared therein. 

Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a) allows a party to apply for leave to 
amend a pleading, and provides that ‘‘leave shall be freely 
given when justice so requires.’’ One authority has observed 
that ‘‘[{s}ubdivision (a) encourages the court to look favora- 
bly on requests to amend .. . . This proposition has been 
reinforced by innumerable judicial pronouncements by the 
federal appellate and district courts alike.’’ 6 Charles A. 
Wright, Arthur R. Miller, & Mary K. Kane, Federal Prac- 
tice and Procedure § 1483 (1990). Most significantly, the 
United States Supreme Court declared in Foman v. Davis, 
371 U.S. 178 (1962), that 


If the underlying facts or circumstances relied upon by 
a plaintiff may be a proper subject of relief, he ought to 
be afforded an opportunity to test his claim on the 
merits. In the absence of any apparent or declared 
reason . . . the leave sought should, as the rules re- 
quire, be ‘‘freely given.”’ 


Id., 371 U.S. at 182. 


B. Plaintiffs’ Revised Third Amended Complaint 
Does Not Prejudice Defendants. 


In its Order this Court expressed concern that Plaintiffs 
had earlier opportunities to add new parties and allege new 
claims, yet they had not done so. The Court did not indicate, 
however, that Defendants were thereby prejudiced. And in 
fact Plaintiffs were not prejudiced by that proposed Third 
Amended Complaint. 


JA-325 


Mere delay, absent significant prejudice to the opposing 
party, is not legally adequate grounds for denying a motion 
to amend. ‘‘It should be emphasized . . . that while laches 
and unexpected delay may bar a proposed amendment, delay 
alone, regardless of its length, is not enough to bar it if the 
other party is not prejudiced.’’ 3 James W. Moore, Richard 
D. Freer, Moore's Federal Practice { 15.08[4], at 15-76 (2d 
ed. 1991) (emphasis added). If that were not true, then any 
motion to amend filed after some indeterminate period of 
delay might be denied for that reason alone, effectively 
imposing a deadline on motions to amend. Nothing in the 
Federal Rules of Civil Procedure indicates its drafters in- 
tended a restriction of that sort. 

The Ninth Circuit has consistently reaffirmed this princi- 
ple. The leading decision is Howey v. United States, 481 
F.2d 1187 (9th Cir. 1973). In Howey the Plaintiff had 
delayed for five years in filing its motion to amend. As in 


the motion entirely by reason of the delay in 
amending. On appeal, the Ninth Circuit reversed, stating: 


‘*The crucial factor is the resulting prejudice to the opposing 
party.”” Id. at 1190. ‘“We discern from the record that the 


moved at an earlier time to amend does not by itself consti- 
tute an adequate basis for denying leave to amend.’’ Jd. at 
1191. 


[W]e know of no case where delay alone was deemed 
Sufficient grounds to deny a Rule 15(a) motion to 
amend. Where there is a lack of prejudice to the oppos- 
ing party and the amended complaint is obviously not 
frivolous, or made as a dilatory maneuver in bad faith, 
it is an abuse of discretion to deny such a motion. The 
purpose of the litigation process is to vindicate merito- 
rious claims. Refusing, solely because of delay, to 


JA-326 


permit an amendment to a pleading in order to state a 
potentially valid claim would hinder this purpose while 
not promoting any other sound judicial policy. Only 
where prejudice is shown or the movant acts in bad 
faith are courts protecting the judicial system or other 
litigants when they deny leave to amend a pleading. 

Id. at 1190-91 (citations omitted) (emphasis added). 


The Ninth Circuit held firm to that principle in United 
States v. Webb, 655 F.2d 977 (9th Cir. 1981) and Hurn v. 
Retirement Fund Trust of Plumbing, Heating and Piping 
Indus. of S. Cal., 648 F.2d 1252 (9th Cir. 1981). In Webb 
the court issued its ringing declaration that 

In exercising [its] discretion, a court must be guided by 
the underlying purpose of Rule 15—to facilitate deci- 
sion on the merits, rather than on the pleadings or 
technicalities. Accordingly, Rule 15’s policy of favor- 
ing amendments to pleadings should be applied with 


Id. at 979 (emphasis added) (citations omitted). The court 
then reaffirmed the holding in Howey, citing as well 
Professor Moore’s treatise: ‘“The mere fact that an amend- 
ment is offered late in the case is . . . not enough to bar it.”’ 
Id. at 980 (citation omitted). 


In Sierra Club v. Union Oil Co. of Cal., 813 F.2d 1480 
(9th Cir.), vacated on other grounds, 485 U.S. 931 (1987), 
the court relied once more on Howey, noting additionally 
that ‘‘where a defendant is on notice of the facts contained 
in an amendment to a complaint, there is no serious preju- 
dice to defendant in allowing the amendment.’’ Jd. at 1493 
(emphasis added). In both their former and revised Third 
Amended Complaints, Plaintiffs have not alleged any new 
facts other than those set forth in their § 1503 claims. In the 


JA-327 


former version, Plaintiffs merely alleged new legal theories; 
in the revised version, Plaintiffs have not even done that. 

The court in Sierra Club additionally stressed the policy 
reasons for liberally granting motions to amend: 


General considerations of judicial economy also justify 
allowing the amendments. The violations included in 
the proposed amendment relate to the same subject 
matter as the original complaint. Allowing the amend- 
ment will further the federal policy of ‘wrapping in 
one bundle all matters concerning the same subject 


Id. at 1493 (citation omitted). 


And as recently as March of 1991 the Ninth Circuit reit- 
erated, in United States v. Pend Oreille Public Utility Dist. 
No. 1, 926 F.2d 1502 (9th Cir. 1991), that ‘“‘the crucial 
factor is not length of delay, but prejudice.’’ Jd. at 1511 
(citing Howey, inter alia). 

For the foregoing reasons, Plaintiffs request that this 
Court allow them to file their revised Third Amended 
Complaint, without the addition of any new parties or claims 
other than those relating to 18 U.S.C. § 1503. Plaintiffs 
believe Defendants cannot even arguably assert any preju- 
dice that might arise from granting that request, and conse- 
quently this Court ought to grant it in accordance with the 
clear mandate of the Ninth Circuit as set forth herein. 


JA-328

market in Nevada, and was approved for that purpose by the 
Insurance Division of Nevada.

C. On or about August 30, 1991, Defendants falsely 
testified, in the sworn deposition of Defendants’ Direc- 
tor of Underwriting, James W. Keene, that Defendants 
had never maintained a ‘‘Jeopardy Committee.’’ Keene 
in fact knew that Defendants had maintained a Jeopardy 
Committee over a period of several years, that he had 
routinely communicated with members of that commit- 
tee, and that the committee had generated numerous 
documents and records. The significance of Defendants’ 
Jeopardy Committee is that it was authorized to reduce 
premiums for group health insurance below the levels 
dictated by normal actuarial principles, and in flagrant 
disregard of the rating methodology that Humana Insur- 
ance represented to the Insurance Division of Nevada as 
charged. The intervention of the Jeopardy Committee 
thereby allowed Humana Insurance to sell or renew 
policies of group health insurance that Humana Insur- 
ance was otherwise in jeopardy of not selling or renew- 
ing. In addition, the intervention allowed Humana 
Insurance to set premiums so low that Humana Insur- 
ance operated at a loss, in violation of Nevada insur- 
aine regulations.

Amended Complaint in response to the rulings in that Order.

JA-382 


Il 
STATEMENT OF THE LAW 


Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a) allows a party to apply for leave to 
amend a pleading, and provides that ‘‘leave shall be freely 
given when justice so requires.’’ One authority has observed 
that ‘‘[s]ubdivision (a) encourages the court to look favora- 
bly on requests to amend... . . This proposition has been 
reinforced by innumerable judicial pronouncements by the 
federal appellate and district courts alike.’’ 6 Charles A. 
Wright, Arthur R. Miller, & Mary K. Kane, Federal Prac- 
tice and Procedure § 1483 (1990). Most significantly, the 
United States Supreme Court declared in Foman v. Davis, 
371 U.S. 178 (1962), that 


If the underlying facts or circumstances relied upon by 
a plaintiff may be a proper subject of relief, he ought to 
be afforded an opportunity to test his claim on the 
merits. In the absence of any apparent or declared 
reason . . . the leave sought should, as the rules re- 
quire, be ‘‘freely given.’ 


Id., 371 U.S. at 182. See also, e.g., United States v. Webb, 
655 F.2d 977 (9th Cir. 1981); Hurn v. Retirement Fund 
Trust, Etc., 648 F.2d 1252 (9th Cir. 1981). in Howey v. 
United States, 481 F.2d 1187 (9th Cir. 1973), the Ninth 
Circuit reversed a denial of leave to amend, even five years 
after the initial complaint was filed, in the absence of preju- 
dice to the defendant. 

Plaintiffs’ proposed Third Amended Complaint is addi- 
tionally a supplemental pleading pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 
15(d). One of the reasons for the adoption of paragraph (d) 
is that otherwise a plaintiff might be forced into 
‘‘commencing a new action even though events occurring 
after the commencement of the original action have made 
clear the right to relief.’’ Fed. R. Civ. P. 15 advisory 
committee notes (1963). That is precisely the situation here. 


JA-383 


1. Allegations regarding Defendants’ RICO conspiracy. 

Plaintiffs file this Third Amended Complaint to allege 
violations of 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d), i.e., a conspiracy to 
violate 18 U.S.C. §§ 1962(a) and (c) of the federal RICO 
statutes. In its Order of July 9, 1991, this court dismissed a 
portion of Plaintiffs’ RICO claims pursuant to 18 U.S.C. 
§ 1962(a). The Court adopted the rule of some federal cir- 
cuits that Plaintiffs were required to establish an injury by 
reason of the use or investment of racketeering income. The 
Court concluded that Plaintiffs ‘have not alleged injury 
resulting from investment of racketeering income, and 
could not do so even if they had the chance to amend.”’ 
Order at 38. 


_ On August 16, 1990, after Plaintiffs had filed both thei 
intial Complaint and their first Amended Complaint, the 
Ninth Circuit issued an opinion in Reddy v. Litton Indus. 
Inc., 912 F.2d 291 (9th Cir. 1990), cert. denied, 60 
U.S.L.W. 3057 (October 15, 1991) that addressed the re- 
quirements for establishing a conspiracy to violate 18 
U.S.C. 1962(a), pursuant to the conspiracy portion of the 
RICO statutes, 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d). The Court held: ‘*[A] 
plaintiff must have been injured by overt acts that are them- 
selves statutorily defined acts of racketeering in order to 
have standing under § 1962(d),”’ id. at 295—i.e., by the 
predicate act itself, and not necessarily by the use or invest- 
ment of racketeering income. In other words, the injury 
requirement for alleging a conspiracy to violate 18 U.S.C. 
Massey (b), or (Cc) leapfrogs those intermediate provi- 

concentrates on the predicate act itself. According- 
ly Plaintiffs now wish to amend their Complaint to allege 
ro age conspired to violate 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a) 


The proposed Third Amended Complaint additionally 
joins two culpable parties to this litigation: the estate of 
Wendell Cherry and its administrator, and William Larry 
Cash. Plaintiffs’ grounds for joining those Defendants are 
set forth at length in the attached Third Amended Complaint 
5-6 and passim. 


3. Absence of prejudice to Defendants. 
Granting Plaintiffs leave to file this Third Amended 
Complaint will not unduly prejudice Defendants, for the 
following reasons: . 


—Plaintiffs allege no new operative facts. The same 
ar  gelationshins. and injuries that 
animated Plaintiffs’ earlier complaints remain at the core of 
this Third Amended Complaint. Hence this litigation will 
not assume any essential new character by reason of the 
allegations set forth in this proposed Third Amended 
Complaint. 
—The additional co-Defendants were knowing, willful, 
and active participants in the scheme to defraud. Hence they 
were aware of the transactions, occurrences, relationships, 
and injuries alleged in this litigation even long before 
Plaintiffs filed their initial Complaint. Moreover, those 
additional co-Defendants were aware of this litigation itself 
from the outset thereof and of Plaintiffs’ allegations therein. 
—The parties to this litigation liave engaged in relatively 
few essential depositions. By far the larger portion of those 
essential depositions remain to be scheduled. Plaintiffs alone 
presently contemplate scheduling more than twenty-five 


JA-385 
_—More than three months remain in this litigation for 
discovery efforts and motions. That is entirely adequate for 
Defendants to pursue all necessary discovery activities.

DEFENDANTS’ MEMORANDUM OF POINTS AND 
AUTHORITIES IN OPPOSITION TO PLAINTIFFS’ 
MOTION FOR LEAVE TO FILE 
THIRD AMENDED COMPLAINT 


I. INTRODUCTION 


More than two and a half years after commencing this 
litigation, Plaintiffs have moved this Court for leave to file a 


JA-390 


seeks to add new parties and new claims, and to revise the 
old claims against Defendants. Unfortunately, these amend- 
ments would alter the course and the pace of this action 
‘onificant! 


Although the standards for leave to amend are liberal, 
leave should not be granted in every situation. Leave should 
be denied when it will prejudice the defendants or unduly 
delay the action without justification. Here, Defendants 
would be prejudiced because the Third Amended Complaint 
two and a half years to narrow and refine the scope of Piain- 
would substantially delay this litigation by generating a 
renewed motion to dismiss and a renewed discovery process. 
to their pleadings because the matters contained in the Third 
Amended Complaint generally could have been asserted in 
Plaintiffs’ earlier pleadings and now will only cause further 
delay. It is time to move forward with this case to an adjudi- 
cation on the merits. Defendants are eager for vindication in 
only delay the disposition of Plaintiffs’ claims. Accordingly, 
Plaintiffs’ motion for leave to amend should be denied. 


fl. STATEMENT OF FACTS 


On March 29, 1989, Plaintiffs filed their original com- 
plaint with this Court, alleging claims under the Employee 
Retirement Income Security Act (‘‘ERISA*’), the Sherman 
Antitrust Act and various provisions of state law. On 
September 27, 1989, the Court dismissed Plaintiffs’ state 
law claims and directed them to file an Amended Complaint 
re-asserting the ERISA and antitrust counts. Plaintiffs filed 
the Amended Complaint on October 27, 1989, alleging 
claims under ERISA and section 2 of the Sherman Antitrust 


JA-391 


claims under the Racketeer Influenced and 
Organizations Act (*‘RICO’’). On July 9, 1991, the 


On October 17, 1991, each Plaintiff's deposition was no- 
ticed in order to probe the bases of the allegations in the 
Second Amended Complaint. On September 11, 1991, all 
counsel met to confer on the discovery issues raised in the 
case. As a result of this meeting, the Court ordered the 
parties to complete all discovery by January 31, 1992. This 
order was based on the parties’ September 18, 1991 status 
report to the Court which was prepared by both sides. At no 
point before or during the discovery conference did Plain- 
tiffs indicate that they expected to amend their complaint a 


First, the Third Amended Complaint attempts to join as 
defendants the Estate of Wendell Cherry (‘‘Cherry’’), the 
late president, chief operating officer, and director of 
Defendant Humana Inc.; and William Larry Cash 
(““Cash’’), an officer and director of Defendant Humana 


prejudice Defendants and unduly prolong this action, Plain- 
tiffs’ motion for leave to amend should be denied. 


' Plaintiffs’ amended RICO claim does include a new allegation that 
Defendants have committed an obstruction of justice under 18 U.S.C. § 


disputes into an additional cause of 


ence with a pending jury trial ongoing in state court. Yet they make no 
such request with respect to the Third Amended Complaint, containing
