JA-393 


A. This Court Has The Discretionary Power To Deny Piaintiffs’ 
Motion For |... sve To File Their Third Amended Complaint. 


Although Fed. &. Civ. P. 15(a) states that leave to amend 
a pleading ‘‘shall be freely given when justice so requires,”’ 
the mere request for leave to amend does not create an 
entitlement to relief. Instead, the power to grant or deny 
leave is within the discretion of the district court. Foman v. 
Davis, 371 U.S. 178, 182 (1962). Under this standard, the 
district courts have developed several grounds for evaluating 
a request for leave to amend. First, ‘‘the district court's 
discretion to deny leave to amend is particularly broad where 
the court has already given the plaintiff an opportunity to 
amend his complaint.’ See Fidelity Financial Corp. v. 
Federal Home Loan Bank of San Francisco, 792 F.2d 1432, 
1438 (9th Cir. 1986), cert. denied, 479 U.S. 1064 (1987). 


Second, a court may properly deny leave to amend ‘‘if 
permitting an amendment would prejudice the opposing 
party, produce an undue delay in the litigation, or result in 
futility for lack of merit.’’ Jackson v. Bank of Hawaii, 902 
F.2d 1385, 1387 (9th Cir. 1990). See generally Ascon 
Properties, Inc. v. Mobile Oil Co., 866 F.2d 1149 (9th Cir. 
1989) (court denied leave for a third time because defendant 
would suffer prejudice); McGlinchy v. Shell Chemical Co.., 
845 F.2d 802 (9th Cir. 1988) (leave denied, in part, because 


JA-394 
originally. Allen, 911 F.2d at 374; McGlinchy, 845 F.2d at 
809. 


Using these three standards, this Court should reject Plain- 
tiffs’ attempt to file their Third Amended Complaint. 
Plaintiffs’ primary justification for their Third Amended 
Complaint is the decision of the Ninth Circuit in Reddy v 
Litton Industries, Inc., 912 F.2d 291 (9th Cir. 1990), cert. 
denied, 60 U.S.L.W. 3288 (1991). In this decision,

recent opinion Plaintiffs imply it is. Plaintiffs have had

to seek leave to amend their complaint to allege a RI 
conspiracy in violation of § 1962(d). In fact, the 
decision was one year old when Plaintiffs filed their

B. Plaintiffs Should Be Denied Leave To Join The Estate 
Of Wendell Cherry And William Larry Cash As Defendants. 


F.2d 977, 980 (9th Cir. 1981); Hurn v. Retirement Fund Trust, Etc.. 
648 F.2d 1252, 1254 (9th Cir. 1981); Howey v. United States, 481 F.2d 
1187, 1190-91 (9th Cir. 1973). Here, by contrast, Defendants have 


JA-396 


Furthermore, the participation of Cherry and Cash as 
Defendants is not necessary to permit Plaintiffs to pursue 
their theories of liability. There is no legal necessity to name 
each of the alleged conspirators as defendants in order to 
recover from any other participant in the purported con- 
spiracy. 

One can only speculate as to why, two and a half years 
after this lawsuit was filed, Plaintiffs would now elect to 
label the recently-deceased president of one corporate 
Defendant and the vice president of the other as 
‘*racketeers.’’* This Court may exercise its discretion to 
avoid this ummecessary consequence of the requested 
amendment, with absolutely no prejudice to Plaintiffs’ right 
to prosecute their claims. 

Additionally, if the proposed amendments are evaluated 
on a purely legal basis, leave to amend as to Cherry and 
Cash should be denied for the reasons set forth below. 


1. Prejudice to Defendants. 


Leave to amend may be denied when it will prejudice the 
defendants. See Jackson, 902 F.2d at 1387; McGlinchy, 845 
F.2d at 809; Ascon, 866 F.2d at 1160-61. In the instant 
case, Cherry and Cash will clearly be prejudiced if they are 
joined as parties. These individuals were never placed on 
notice of any potential individual liability when Plaintiffs 


‘ Plaintiffs, in their Ninth-Request for Production of Documents di- 
rected to Defendants, sought material from the Estate of Wendell Cher- 
ry. One can only hope that the decision to name Cherry’s Estate is not an 
improper attempt to facilitate discovery or an unseemly prelude to a 
litigation strategy designed to exert emotional pressure upon the corpo- 
rate Defendants through active litigation against the Estate. See Webb, 
655 F.2d at 980 (bad faith amendment grounds for denying leave to 
amend). See also Hurn, 648 F.2d at 1254; Howey, 481 F.2d at 1190-91. 


JA-397 


filed their earlier pleadings. It is manifestly unjust for these 
individuals now to be joined in litigation already two and a 
half years old; the action has taken a shape over which they 
had no control. 

This is particularly true as to Cherry. Plaintiffs have 
known of the activities of Cherry at all times during this 
litigation, and waited until Cherry was deceased to name 
him as a party. This loss of access to a potentially vital 


witness is clearly prejudicial. See Boris v. Moore, 253 F.2d 
523, 524 (7th Cir. 1958). 


2. Delay of the Litigation. 


Leave to amend also may be denied when it will result in 
a delay of the litigation. See Ascon, 866 F.2d at 1160-16 
(leave denied because defendant would suffer prejudice due 
to substantial litigation costs already incurred and the pros- 
pect of additional time, expense and discovery); Lewis v. 
Berry, 109 F.R.D. 42, 43 (W.D. Wash. 1985) (additional 
and a postponement of trial which would be extremely 

udicial to parties). 

Here, the addition of the individual Defendants will 
ee eee Ow aen For example, Cherry and 

Cash likely will move to dismiss the Third Amended 
Complaint.* Moreover, the addition of these individuals 


* The liability of corporate officers for the activities of the corporation 
they serve is a real issue that must be examined by the Court. For 
example, the primary emphasis of Plaintiffs’ new RICO theory is that the 
corporate Defendants, Cherry and Cash conspired to violate sections 
1962(a) and 1962(c) of RICO, in violation of section 1962(d). But this 
attempt to impute liability is not supportable. No Ninth Circuit appellate 
cases have addressed the issue of whether a properly pled RICO conspir- 
acy under § 1962(d) may consist solely of a corporation, its officers, em-

would expand the scope of the litigation and necessarily 
increase the time required to complete discovery without in 
any manner advancing Plaintiffs’ theories of liability or 
facilitating proof of their claims. 


3. Absence of New Facts. 


A court may deny leave when the movant presents no new 
facts, only new theories based upon facts previously in 


ployees or subsidiaries. However, in a RICO case more recent than the 
Reddy case upon which Plaintiffs rely, and one which cites Reddy, the 
District Court in U.S. Concord, Inc. v. Harris Graphics Corp., 757 F 
Supp. 1053, 1060-61 (N.D. Cal. 1991), specifically ruled that a corpora- 
tion may not be held liable under § 1962(d) for a conspiracy involving it, 
its employees and directors. This ruling follows the majority of District 
of Nova Scotia, 653 F. Supp. 1278, 1287 (D.V.1. 1987); H.G. Galli- 
more, Inc. v. Abdula, 652 F. Supp. 437, 447 n.5 (N.D. Ill. 1987); 
Gaudette v. Panos, 650 F. Supp. 912, 913-14 (D. Mass. 1987), rev'd on 
other grounds, 852 F.2d 30 (ist Cir. 1988); Satellite Financial Planning 
Corp. v. First Nat'l Bank of Wilmington, 633 F. Supp. 386, 405 n.23 
(D. Del. 1986); Medallion TV Enterprises, Inc. v. SelecTV of California, 
Inc., 627 F. Supp. 1290, 1301 n.7 (C.D. Cal. 1986), aff'd, 833 F.2d 
1360 (9th Cir. 1988), cert. denied, 109 S. Ct. 3241 (1989); McLendon 
v. Continental Group, Inc., 602 F. Supp. 1492, 1510-12 (D. N.J. 
1985); with Pandick, Inc. v. Rooney, 632 F. Supp. 1430, 1435-36 (N.D. 
Ill. 1986); Callan v. State Chemical Mfg. Co., Inc., 584 F. Supp. 619, 
623 (E.D. Pa. 1984); Saine v. AIA, Inc., 582 F. Supp. 1299, 1307 (D. 
Colo. 1984). Only one circuit court has held that a conspiracy between a 
corporation and its employees may be proved, but under circumstances 
different from those alleged here. See Ashland Oil, Inc. v. Arnett, 875 
F.2d 1271, 1281 (7th Cir. 1989). No other circuit courts have addressed 
the issue. Consequently, the attempt to include Cherry and Cash is legal- 
ly defective. See Foman, 371 U.S. at 182 (futility of amendment is 
factor in denying leave to amend). See also Webb, 655 F.2d at 980; 
Hurn, 648 F.2d at 1254; Howey, 481 F.2d at 1190-91. More generally, 
there are a variety of other defenses which can be raised by Cherry and 
Cash which were not at issue until now. 


911 F.2d at 374 (leave denied because movant presented no 
new facts, only a different legal theory); Fidelity, 792 F.2d

_ Accordingly, the Court should deny Plaintiffs iffs leave to 
join Cherry and Cash as defendants. 


C. Plaintiffs Should Be Denied Leave To Add Additional Claims 
Against The Original Parties To This Litigation. 


_ Plaintiffs also seek to amend their claims against the exist- 
ing corporate Defendants. As noted above, this motion is 
based exclusively on the year-old decision cf the Ninth 
Circuit in Reddy which, at best, is relevant only to an 
amendment of Plaintiffs’ new RICO conspiracy claims. 


JA-400 


However, without informing the Court in their motion, 
Plaintiffs have completely rewritten their claims under both 
ERISA and section 2 of the Sherman Act in their Third 
Amevied Complaint; they have added a claim under Neva- 
da’s ‘‘littlhe RICO’ statute; and they have even revised the 
RICO claim under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(c), which the Court 
permitted Plaintiffs to pursue in its July 9, 1991 Order. 
This tack is highly questionable because the significance 
of these proposed changes, especially to the ERISA and 
antitrust counts, is uncertain. Even after a detailed 
comparison of the allegations of the pending Second 
Amended Complaint and the claims set forth in the proposed 
Third Amended Complaint, it is not clear whether Plaintiffs 
intena to change the substance of their claims under ERISA 
and the antitrust laws. Before leave is granted, Plaintiffs 
should be required to answer these questions. Due to the 
existing ambiguities in Plaintiffs’ proposed claims, however, 
the corporate Defendants are obliged to oppose the motion 
for leave to file the Third Amended Complaint. 


1. Prejudice to Defendants. 


The Court should deny Plaintiffs’ leave to file the Third 
Amended Complaint because the amendment will substan- 
tially prejudice the corporate Defendants. See Jackson, 902 
F.2d at 1387; McGlinchy, 845 F.2d at 809; Ascon, 866 F.2d 
at 1161 (leave to amend denied because filing of third 
complaint would prejudice defendants). A review of the 
facts demonstrates that prejudice would result if leave is 
granted. 

First, defendants have endeavored over the past two and a 
half years to narrow and refine the scope of Plaintiffs’ 
pleadings so that Defendants could prepare their defense to 
cognizable claims. Expending significant resources, Defend- 
ants have twice moved to dismiss Plaintiffs’ claims for 


JA-401 


pleading deficiencies which the Court recognized. However, 
after Plaintiffs filed their Second Amended Complaint, De- 
fendants believed the issues to be adjudicated in this litiga- 
tion had finally been framed and a course of discovery was 
charted. Now Plaintiffs request leave to file an amended 
pleading that will force Defendants to relitigate additional 
dispositive motions. 

Second, Defendants have already propounded extensive 
written discovery tailored to ascertain the basis for particular 
allegations in the Second Amended Complaint. This discov- 
ery would be rendered moot by the Third Amended Com- 
plaint, forcing Defendants to issue new discovery requests. 
For these reasons, Defendants will be significantly preju- 
diced if this Court grants Plaintiffs leave to file the Third 
Amended Complaint. 

Third, the timing of Plaintiffs’ motion for leave to amend 
severely prejudices the corporate Defendants’ ability to 
gather information relevant to Plaintiffs’ claims. For the first 
time in two and a half years, Plaintiffs have suggested that 
Wendell Cherry was somehow uniquely involved in the 
alleged RICO conspiracies. Prior to Cherry’s death, howev- 
er, Plaintiffs not only refrained from making any such alle- 
gations, they did not even notice Cherry's deposition to 
determine if such allegations were proper. Plaintiffs’ litiga- 
tion strategy has effectively prevented Defendants from 
btaining inf ling the alleged activities giving 
rise to liability from the best, if not, only source: Mr. 
Cherry himself. 


2. Delay of the Litigation. 


This Court also should deny Plaintiffs leave to amend 
because the filing of the Third Amended Complaint will 
unduly delay the litigation. See Ascon, 866 F.2d at 1161; 
Lewis, 109 F.R.D. at 43. 


JA-402 


In a case of this magnitude, there is a need to gain control 
of the facts and issues within a reasonable time. Thus, the 
Court has established a discovery cut-off date. The parties 
have already embarked on a pattern of discovery in an effort 
to meet the Court’s discovery deadline. An amendment to 
Plaintiffs’ complaint at this time would require another 
motion to dismiss and additional discovery that probably 
could not be completed within the current discovery period. 

Moreover, Plaintiffs’ addition of a state RICO claim and 
federal RICO conspiracy claims to this class action requires 
a new class certification procedure pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. 
P. 23(c)(1). See Zenith Laboratories, Inc. v. Carter- 
Wallace, Inc., 64 F.R.D. 159, 164 (D.N.J. 1974), aff'd, 
530 F.2d 508 (3d Cir.), cert. denied, 429 U.S. 828 (1976). 
If these new claims were certified for class action treatment, 
class members would require a new notice of their right to 
opt out of the litigation. See Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(c)(2); Mata- 
razzo v. Friendly Ice Cream Corp., 70 F.R.D. 536, 560 
(E.D.N.Y. 1976). The delay caused by this procedure 
warrants the denial of leave to amend. See Matarazzo, 70 
F.R.D. at 560 (leave to amend denied because of delay 
caused by need to deliver new opt-out notice). 


In sum, the addition of the claims set forth in the Third 
; ted Complai Id delay this litigation significantly. 


3. Absence of New Facts. 


This Court has the discretion to deny leave to amend 
when the movant attempts to change its theories of liability 
without alleging the occurrence of any new facts to support 
such theories. See Allen, 911 F.2d at 374. 

Plaintiffs seek to expand their ERISA claims by adding 
alleged instances of misconduct by Defendants. For exam- 
ple, Plaintiffs contend that Defendants submitted a 
fraudulent master policy to the Insurance Division of Neva- 


With respect to their RICO claim, Plaintiffs propose to 
modify their definitions itions of the RICO ‘‘persons’’ and the 


leave to add a Fourth Claim for Relief alleging violations of 
Nevada racketeering laws. This state law claim could have 


plaints.

POINTS AND AUTHORITIES 
' . 
STATEMENT OF THE CASE 


On March 29, 1989, Plaintiffs filed their initial Complaint 
in this matter. On April 28, 1989, Defendants filed a Motion 
to Dismiss that Complaint. On September 27, 1989, this 
Court entered an Order effectively denying that Motion to 
Dismiss. On October 27, 1989, Plaintiffs filed an Amended 
Complaint in response to the rulings in that Order. On 
January 18, 1990, Defendants filed a Second Motion to 
Dismiss. On July 9, 1991, this Court entered an Order effec- 
tively denying that Second Motion to Dismiss. In that Order 
the Court granted Plaintiffs leave to file a Second Amended 
Complaint, and on August 12, 1991, Plaintiffs filed their 
Second Amended Complaint. On September 4, 1991, 
Defendants filed their first Answer. 


JA-415 


On October 31, 1991, Plaintiffs filed their Motion to File 
Third Amended Complaint and Jury Demand (‘‘Motion’’) 
(Exhibit 1). On November 15, 1991, Defendants filed their 
Opposition (‘‘Opposition’’) (Exhibit 2).

INTRODUCTION 


Five compelling reasons for granting the proposed 
Tid Acta’ Giaghiag. 


1. The overwhelming weight of authority has since 
salliiehed tar Wate ie died IC oh eee 
liberality. In opposing Plaintiffs’ Motion, Defendants have 
cited merely a few scattered decisions where courts denied 
leave to amend only after dismissing the plaintiffs ’ claims or 

2. Defendants offered no grounds for assuming prejudice 
to themselves. Any delay that Plaintiffs’ proposed Amend- 
ment might occasion is simply inconsequential by 
comparison with the enormous delays that Defendants 
themselves have brought to this matter. 


3. Defendants are not even in a position to argue, on 
behalf of Cherry and Cash, that Plaintiffs’ proposed Third 


i the

Cash. To the contrary, its timing may well benefit them 
allowing Cherry and Cash to save thousands of dollars by

Inc., 912 F.2d 291 (9th Cir. 1990), cert. denied, 60 
U.S.L.W. 3057 (October 15, 1991), a decision issued well 
after Plaintiffs filed their first Amended Complaint and one 
that the United States Supreme Court refused to overturn 
only after Plaintiffs filed their Second Amended Complaint. 


5. Nor have Defendants even attempted to offer any 
grounds for this Court to deny Plaintiffs an opportunity to 
recover for Defendants’ violations of 18 U.S.C. § 
1503—once more, violations that occurred or that Plaintiffs 
discovered only after filing their Second Amended Com- 
plaint. 

Accordingly the path is clear for this Court to grant 
Plaintiffs’ Motion; and, in the interests of both justice and 
judicial economy, it may do so without reservation. 


Il 
ARGUMENT 
1. Leave to Amend is Granted with Extreme Liberality 
The most compelling observation on granting leave to 
amend a complaint that has issued from the Ninth Circuit 
appeared in United States v. Webb, 655 F.2d 977 (9th Cir. 
1981): 


In exercising this discretion, a court must be guided by 
the underlying purpose of Rule 15—to facilitate deci- 
sion on the merits, rather than on the pleadings or 
technicalities. Accordingly, Rule 15's policy of favor- 
ing amendments to pleadings should be applied with 


Id. at 979 (emphasis added) (citations omitted). 


where a court denied leave to amend after refusing to dis- 
miss the action—or, for that matter, after twice refusing to 
dismiss the action, as in the present instance. 


In the very first decision cited by Defendants, Foman v.

to amend. That is the reason Plaintiffs themselves cited

2. The Proposed Third Amended Complaint is a Natural 
Evolutionary Development in This Litigation 


A broader perspective on the course of this litigation may 
be in order. On October 27, 1989, after this Court had 
denied Defendants’ first Motion to Dismiss, Plaintiffs filed 
their first Amended Complaint. On November 17, 1989, 
Plaintiffs stipulated to allow Defendants an additional 15 
days to file their Answer by December 12, 1989. On 
December 13, 1989, Plaintiffs stipulated to allow Defend- 


JA-420 


ants an additional five weeks to file their Answer by January 
18, 1990. Finally, on January 18, 1990, more than two and 
a half months after Plaintiffs had filed their first Amended 
Compliant, Defendants filed, instead of an Answer, a 
Second Motion to Dismiss. 


Defendants disputed the issues in that Second Motion to 
Dismiss. By the time this Court denied that Motion, nearly 
two and a half years had passed in this litigation and De- 
fendants had not yet even filed an Answer. 


This Court held a hearing on that Second Motion to 
Dismiss on September 25, 1990, scarcely one month after 
the decision in Reddy, and Reddy had not yet come to the 
attention of Plaintiffs at the time of that hearing. Defendants 
now contend that Plaintiffs were obligated to amend their 
the Second Motion to Dismiss, rather than awaiting the 
Court’s ruling on that Motion. After the interminable delays 
caused by Defendants’ two Motions to Dismiss, however, 
Plaintiffs wanted to resolve those issues and finally obtain an 
Answer. 

By the time this Court denied that Motion and granted 
Plaintiffs leave to file a Second Amended Complaint, Plain- 
tiffs were considering a variety of amendments that have 
now surfaced in their proposed Third Amended Complaint. 
Plaintiffs regarded the Second Amended Complaint as 
merely an interim effort, filed principally to cure a defect in 
the First Amended Complaint and to compel an Answer 
from Defendants. 

Plaintiffs then enlisted the assistance of nine attorneys and 
numerous experts from across the United States to draft a 
stand any prospective scrutiny, in this Court or on any level 
of appeal. That process required rather more than the one 


JA-421 


month available to Plaintiffs for filing their Second 
Amended Complaint. Plaintiffs nonetheless managed to 
complete the proposed Third Amended Complaint in slightly 
more than three months.

nullify Defendants’ efforts over the past two and a half years 
to narrow and refine the scope of Plaintiffs’ pleadings,’’ 
Opposition at 2; see also id. at 14. Yet in many respects the

b. Cherry and Cash Are Appropriate Candidates 
for Plaintiffs’ Proposed Amendment 


As executive officers and directors of Humana and 
Humana Insurance, Cherry and Cash had a close and even 
intimate relationship with the present Defendants. One 
authority has observed that ‘‘plaintiffs have been 
allowed . . . to add or substitute parties where there is an 
extremely close corporate or other relationship between the 
original and added defendant, and accrues no prejudice to 
the new party,”’ 3 James W. Moore, Richard D. Freer, 
Moore’s Federal Practice | 15.05{5] (2d ed. 1991). Courts 
have so allowed in the first circuit (see, e.g., Ayala Serrano 
v. Collazo Torres, 650 F. Supp. 722 (D.P.R. 1986); the 
second circuit (see, e.g., Holiday Publishing Co. v. Gregg, 


JA-425 


330 F. Supp. 1326 (S.D.N.Y. 1971); the third circuit (see, 
e.g., Gram v. May, 41 F.R.D. 52 (E.D. Pa. 1966); the fifth 
circuit (see, e.g., Wedgwood v. Fiberboard Corp., 706 F.2d 
541 (Sth Cir. 1983); the seventh circuit (see, e.g., Clark v. 
Universal Builders, Inc., 501 F.2d 324 (7th Cir.), cert. 
denied, 419 U.S. 1070 (1974); and the tenth circuit (see, 
e.g., United States for use of Construction Specialties Co. v. 
Travelers Ins. Co., 40 F.R.D. 316 (D. Colo. 1966). The 
only contrary decisions cited in Moore occurred in Pennsyl- 
vania, and the most recent of those was in 1962. In Ayala 
Serrano, the court expressly ruled that ‘‘where the proposed 
party’s interests are sufficiently aligned with those of the 
original defendant, the latter is presumed to give notice of 
Suit to the former.’’ Ayala Serrano, 650 F.Supp. at 726 
(citations omitted).

d. Cherry and Cash Will Not Be Prejudiced 


Defendants contend that ‘‘[Cherry and Cash] were never 
placed on notice of any potential individual liability when 
Plaintiffs filed their earlier pleadings. . . . [T]he action has 
taken a shape over which [Cherry and Cash] had no 
control.’’ Opposition at 10. 


Defendants have not specified how or in what manner 
Cherry and Cash might be prejudiced by this amendment. 
Plaintiffs have merely asserted, without more, that ‘‘Cherry 
and Cash will clearly be prejudiced.’’ Jd. This Court ought 
to resist Defendants’ invitation to assume some abstract 
prejudice in the absence of any evidence or argument indi- 
cating what prejudice, if any, might accrue. 

Defendants have not indicated, for instance, and Plaintiffs 
cannot imagine, precisely how the ‘‘shape’’ of this litigation 
might have changed if Plaintiffs had named Cherry and Cash 
as individual defendants earlier. Every act that Plaintiffs 
have attributed to Cherry and Cash, Plaintiffs have attributed 
to the present Defendants as well. In the process of defend- 
ing Humana and Humana Insurance against those allega- 
tions, opposing counsel has effectively defended Cherry and 
Cash against them as well. 


Moreover, Defendants themselves have consistently 
adopted to position that this litigation is not essentially a 
dispute over facts at all; it is merely a series of disputes over 
legal interpretations and applications. Defendants are thereby 
hoist by their own petards. If this litigation involves merely 
legal interpretations and not facts, then adding Cherry and 
Jones as co-Defendants will not affect those disputes in the 


slightest. 


JA-429

it 1s not because Plaintiffs failed to name Cherry and Cash

4. The Decision in Reddy Offers Ample Grounds 
for Leave to Amend 


Plaintiffs have noted that the decision in Reddy v. Litton 
Indus., Inc., 912 F.2d 291 (9th Cir. 1990), cert. denied, 60 
U.S.L.W. 3057 (October 15, 1991), resolved in Plaintiffs’ 
favor an issue that prevented Plaintiffs from alleging RICO 
violations of 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d) conspiracy to violate 18 


JA-433 


U.S.C. § 1962(a). See Motion at 3-4.‘ Plaintiffs had already 
alleged violations of § 1962(a), and regarded an allegation 
of conspiracy to violate § 1962(a) as entirely duplicative in 
terms of the damages that Plaintiffs might recover. 


Even the decision in Reddy was of no more than academic 
interest to Plaintiffs so long as their § 1962(a) allegations 
remained intact. On July 9, 1991, however, this Court 
dismissed those § 1962(a) allegations, on the grounds that 
Plaintiffs had not demonstrated and could not demonstrate 
injury from Defendants’ use or investment of racketeering 
income. 


At that moment the decision in Reddy acquired new and 
immediate significance to this litigation. Reddy now offered 
Plaintiffs an opportunity to preserve their § 1962(a) claims 
by alleging conspiracy to violate that section, pursuant to 18 
U.S.C. § 1962(d). 

Hence Plaintiffs had no reason to cite Reddy, or to allege 
violations of 18 U.S.C. § 1962(d), prior to this Court's 
Order of July 9, 1991 — less than four months before Plain- 


* Defendants dispute the holding in Reddy, asserting that ‘‘[nJo case 
decided subsequent to Reddy supports Plaintiffs’ interpretation.’ Opposi- 
tion at 7 n.2. That is rather less than surprising, given that the United 
States Supreme Court denied certiorari in Reddy only on October 15, 
1991, less than one month before Defendants filed their Opposition. 


Defendants then cite three decisions, Saul v. United States, 928 F.2d 
829 (9th Cir. 1991), Price v. Hawaii, 921 F.2d 950 (9th Cir. 1990), and 
U.S. Concord, Inc. v. Harris Graphics Corp., 757 F. Supp. 1053 (N.D. 
Cal. 1991). Plaintiffs are at a loss to penetrate Defendants’ reasons for 
those citations. The two decisions from the Ninth Circuit, Saul and 
Price, did not involve RICO allegations at all. In the decision from the 
District Court for the Northern District of California, U.S. Concord, the 
court dismissed RICO claims for reasons having nothing to do with the 
decision in Reddy. In fact, the court’s only references to Reddy did not 
even involve the § 1962(d) conspiracy. 


JA-434 


tiffs filed their motion for leave to amend. Consequently 
Defendants’ assertion that ‘‘Plaintiffs have had nearly six- 
teen months to advise the Court of the Reddy decision, to 
file an amended complaint with the court, and to seek leave 
to amend their complaint,’’ Opposition at 7, is misguided at 
best. 

Finally, in one of the very decisions relied on by Defend- 
ants, Sierra Club v. Union Oil of Calif., 813 F.2d 1480 (9th 
Cir. 1987), the Ninth Circuit reversed a denial of leave to 
amend, holding that ‘‘[mJere delay in proffering an amend- 
ment does not justify denying leave to amend.’’ Jd. at 1493 
(citation omitted). ‘“Where a defendant is on notice of the 
facts contained in an amendment to a complaint, there is no 
serious prejudice to defendant in allowing the amendment.’’ 
Id. Defendants have not even alleged any prejudice by 
allowing this aspect of the proposed amendment, and De- 
fendants cannot so allege. They were already on notice as to 
the facts contained in that amendment. 


5. New Facts Have Arisen Concerning 
Defendants’ Obstruction of Justice

evidence of abuses by Defendants that prompted the filing of 
an entirely new claim for relief, involving obstruction of 
justice in violation of 18 U.S.C. § 1503. Defendants have 
attempted to disparage that new claim as ‘‘elevating these 
procedural skirmishes to a substantive criminal offense in 
this complaint,’’ Opposition at 5 n.1, and as an “‘attempt to 
bootstrap discovery disputes into an additional cause of 
action,”’ id. Plaintiffs cannot concede, however, that viola- 
tions of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, the Racketeer 
Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act, and a federal 
criminal statute qualify as mere ‘‘procedural skirmishes’’ or 


6. Defendants’ Other Objections Are Meritless 


ries of liability. Defendants cite the portion of Plaintiffs’ 
amended ERISA claims alleging ‘‘that Defendants submitted 
a fraudulent master policy to the Insurance Division of 
Nevada,’’ Opposition at 17, and assert: ‘‘This is a new 
theory of recovery.’’ Jd. Defendants have misapprehended 
that portion of the proposed Third Amended Complaint. 
Plaintiffs are not pursuing recovery for fraud on the Insur- 
ance Division of Nevada, nor have Plaintiffs named the 
Insurance Division as a new class member. Plaintiffs are 
merely pursuing, now as before, Defendants’ fraud on the 
Premium-Payor Class. Defendants’ submission of the fraud- 
ulent master policy to the Insurance Division was simply one 
aspect of the process whereby Defendants accomplished that 
fraud. 


* Defendants additionally find ‘‘anomalous’’ that Plaintiffs sought to 
file under seal a Motion for Partial Default involving those allegations, 
“presumably to avoid impermissible interference with a pending jury 
trial ongoing in state court,”’ id., yet Plaintiffs did not file their Motion 
for Leave under seal as well. This Court ought to bear in mind that it 
was Plaintiffs, not Defendants, who sought to preserve the integrity of 
that pending trial. And it was Plaintiffs who concluded that integrity was 
pot significantly threatened by minimal references to those allegations 
buried in a highly technical, 69-page Complaint that was largely incom-

the instant Reply. 


JA-436 


Defendants contend that ‘‘addition of a state RICO claim 
and federal RICO conspiracy claims to this class action 
requires a new class certification procedure pursuant to Fed. 
R. Civ. P.23(c)(1).’’ Opposition at 16. That is simply erro- 
neous. The Nevada RICO Premium-Payor and Co-Payor 
classes will be identical to the federal RICO Premium-Payor 
and Co-Payor Classes. Precisely the same issues of numer- 
osity, commonality, typicality, and adequacy are involved as 
those in regard to the Federal RICO classes — issues that 
were completely briefed, argued, and ruled on by this Court 
in certifying those classes. Hence the Court may readily 
dispose of those issues in certifying Nevada RICO classes. 
The same is true of Plaintiffs’ amended federal RICO con- 
spiracy claims.°® 

Defendants’ only other objections to the Nevada RICO 
claims in Plaintiffs’ proposed Third Amended Complaint are 
that ‘‘[t}his state law claim [sic] could have been asserted in 
Plaintiffs’ original complaint. These amendments amount 
only to shifts in Plaintiffs’ legal theories.’’ Opposition at 17. 
Plaintiffs have not ‘‘shifted’’ their legal theories at all. 
Plaintiffs have added Nevada RICO claims that, as already 
noted supra, are largely coextensive with Plaintiffs’ federal 
RICO claims. 


* In the decision cited by Defendants to argue that additional class 
notices are necessary, Matarazzo v. Friendly Ice Cream Corp., 70

addition of a new claim that characterized the plaintiffs as independent 
contractors instead of franchisees. That situation does not even remotely 
arise here. Moreover, the court in Matarazzo held that if, at trial, the 
problem was shown to add no new burdens to the plaintiffs, then they 
might be granted leave to amend.

reason . . . the leave sought should, as the rules re- 
quire, be ‘‘freely given.’’

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
DISTRICT OF NEVADA 


CASE NO. CV-S-89-249-PMP (LRL) 
MARY FORSYTH, et al. 


vs. 
HUMANA, INC., et al., 


Before the Court is Plaintiffs’ Motion for Leave to File 
Third Amended Complaint and Jury Demand (#490), filed 
October 31, 1991. Defendants filed their Opposition (#508) 
on November 15, 1991. On November 18, 1991, Plaintiffs 
filed a Notice of Errata in Motion to File Third Amended 
Complaint (#509). On November 25, 1991, Plaintiffs filed 
their Reply (#512). 


Having read and considered the papers before it, the 
Court finds that Plaintiffs have failed to demonstrate that the 
proposed additions to the complaint could not have been 
included in earlier amendments. Plaintiffs have had numer- 
ous Opportunities to apprise Defendants and this Court of all 
to bring before the Court. 

IT IS THEREFORE ORDERED that Plaintiffs’ Motion 
for Leave to File Third Amended Complaint and Jury 
Demand (#490) is denied. 


DATED: December 5, 1991 


/s/ Philip M. Pro 
PHILIP M. PRO 
United States District Judge
